iii 
 
PREFACE 
 
I, the Chairman of the Department-related Parliamentary Standing Committee on Health and 
Family Welfare, having been authorized by the Committee to present the Rep ort on its behalf, 
present this 166th Report of the Committee on Action Taken by the Government on the 
Recommendations/ Observations contained in the 1 11th Report on ―Functioning of New AIIMS 
(Phase-I) under Pradhan Mantri Swasthya Suraksha Yojana (PMSSY)‖.  
2. The One Hundred Eleventh Report of the Department -related Parliamentary Standing 
Committee on Health and Family Welfare was presented to the Rajya Sabha and laid on the Table 
of the Lok Sabha on  28th December, 2018. However, due to Covid -19 pandemic and certain 
discrepancies in the Action Taken Note furnished by the Ministry, the preparation of Action Taken 
Report was delayed. The Action Taken Notes  of the Ministry was returned on a number of 
occasions with a request to furnish a consolidated, compreh ensive and updated Action Taken Notes  
on the recommendations contained in the Report which were finally received from the Departments 
in the Month of May, 2025.   
3.  The Committee made a total of 253 recommendations in the 1 11th Report, out of which 145 
recommendations have been accepted by the Government and have been categorized under 
Chapter- I. There are 23 recommendations, which the Committee does not desire to pursue in view 
of the  Government‘s replies that have been categorized under Chapter-II. The re are  78 
recommendations/ observations, in respect of which replies of the Government, have not been 
accepted by the Committee and the Committee has made further recommendations thereon and has 
been categorized under Chapter-III. While 07 recommendations/observations in respect of which 
final replies of the Government have not been received, have been categorized under Chapter-IV. 
4. The Committee, in its meeting held on the 24th September, 2025 considered the Draft Report 
and adopted the same. 
 
 
 
NEW DELHI                            PROF. RAM GOPAL YADAV  
24th September, 2025                                    CHAIRMAN 
Ashwin, 1947 (Saka)     Department-related Parliamentary Standing  
      Committee on Health and Family Welfare

was presented to the House on 28th December, 2018.

1 
 
CHAPTER–I 
 
RECOMMENDATIONS/OBSERVATIONS THAT HAVE BEEN ACCEPTED BY 
THE GOVERNMENT 
 
A. AIIMS BHOPAL 
 
1.1 STATUS OF CONSTRUCTION WORK 
 
Recommendations/Observations  
 
1.1.1 The Committee observes that the delay in the cons truction work was mainly due to 
non-performance by the selected construction agencies along with improper planning and 
ineffective monitoring. Though the construction status is near completion in the first three 
Packages, under Package IV only 60% of the w ork is complete. It is high time that the 
Institute should strengthen the existing infrastructure and strive to complete the remaining 
work within the stipulated time limits as it has been six years since the Institute has been 
operationalized and the cons truction work is still incomplete. The Committee is inclined to 
observe that the selection of construction agencies was devoid of merit and transparency 
resulting in substantial time and cost overrun. The Committee, therefore, recommends that 
stringent monitoring mechanism should be put in place to ensure timely completion of work 
to avoid time and cost overruns. The Committee recommends that the Ministry should take 
necessary steps to take possession of the area which is under encroachment. The Committee is 
concerned to note that the project consultant HLL has miserably failed in proper monitoring 
of the project and its entire approach reflects unprofessionalism. The Committee, therefore, 
recommends suitable remedial action including fixing accountability a nd appropriate action 
keeping in view the observations of the CAG. 
(Para 2.14 of the Report) 
Action Taken 
 
1.1.2 The observations / recommendation of the Hon‘ble Committee has been noted for 
future guidance. It is submitted that the main reasons for the de lay in construction was due to 
preparation of erroneous Bills of Quantities (BoQ), delays in issue of drawings by original 
design consultants and delays in clearance of deviations, extra items and substituted items by 
original Project Consultant. The progr ess of DPR works is being reviewed regularly at the 
Institute level and in the Ministry, through the online dashboard.  The progress of DPR works 
of all the AIIMS is also reviewed in the CIB meetings. Considerable progress has been 
achieved since the visit made by the Committee. The present progress under various packages 
of civil construction work as in December‘ 2021 is as below: 
Sl.no. Package Status of work as on 
13.12.2021 
Expected date of Completion 
1 Package - I 100% Completed 
2 Package - II 100% Completed 
3 Package - III 100% Contract of M/s C&S Electric Ltd has 

2 
 
been determined on 02.08.2019 due to 
non-performance and balance minor 
works undertaken at Institute level 
4 (a)  Package – IV  40.00% Terminated 
(b)  Balance work 100% Completed 
 
1.1.3 The Work for Estate Services under Package IV was awarded to M/s IVRCL which 
could execute only 40 % of the assigned scope of work, got terminated on 10.08.2016 due to 
non-performance. Subsequently, the balance work of Estate Services (Package IV) was 
assigned to CPWD, which completed the balance scope of 60%. 
1.1.4 The horticulture work has also been completed.  The system of assigning the 
construction work to executing agencies on turnkey basis with empowerment to handle all 
contractual matters at their end has been introduced. The executing agencies have been 
mandated to deploy and use Project Management Software to bring about improved project 
management at their end. The Ministry has also developed an online dashboard to facilitate a 
closer and more ef fective monitoring. The functional AIIMS have been empowered with 
greater delegation of powers in construction, procurement upto Rs 5.00 crore in cases and also 
in administrative matters. The matter regarding removal of encroachment and taking 
possession of the balance land from the state Govt will be persuaded with the State Govt. 
 
Recommendations/Observations  
 
1.1.5 The Committee fails to understand that no cost -benefit analysis was undertaken by the 
Institute before procuring medical equipment in AIIMS,  Bhopal. The Committee wonders 
how AIIMS, Bhopal procured equipment without ascertaining the availability of space, 
manpower and infrastructure required for installation , etc. The Committee, therefore, 
recommends to the Ministry that proper assessment with  respect to cost -benefit analysis 
should be done before the purchase of any equipment. 
(Para 2.19 of the Report) 
Action Taken 
 
1.1.6 The observation / recommendation of the Hon‘ble Committee has been noted for  
future guidance. As the institute is being est ablished, the procurement of medical equipment 
is being done for the first time and is in line with usual equipment available in similar 
departments across other similar INIs. No formal cost -benefit analysis has been done for 
procurement of medical equipme nt. However, whenever department requests for an 
equipment, it does an internal exercise regarding its requirement / essentiality patient care. 
The assessment committee, then thoroughly evaluates the cost justification, the proposed 
benefit for healthcare and a proper justification for expenditure over other competing 
requirements of other departments. It is only after a thorough deliberation & discussion that 
the assessment committee approves the purchase of each and every equipment.  

3 
 
1.1.7 All Procurement is rationalized based on multiple level of assessment such as proper 
justification, Need assessment, utilization, life cycle cost and thoroughly examined at each 
level with the existing committees in place such as SPC, Prioritization, infrastructure 
rationalization committee, negotiation committee etc. Further, the pre offer inspection and site 
readiness certificate has been ensured before the supply in order to mitigate the delay in 
installation and commissioning the equipment. 
Recommendations/Observations  
 
1.1.8 The Committee would like to be apprised of the circumstances under which the 
process and procedure of single bid system was adhered to in purchasing three equipment viz. 
(i) endobronchial ultrasound system (ii) Portable RO/WTU and (iii) Hemodialy sis machine. 
The Committee, in this connection, strongly recommends for procurement of equipment on 
multiple bidding basis in future so as to have fair competitive pricing and availability of more 
options for procurement of best and economical equipment. 
(Para 2.21 of the Report) 
Action Taken 
 
1.1.9 The observation/recommendation of the Hon‘ble Committee has been noted for future 
guidance. It is submitted that the new procurement policy announced by Ministry in March 
2017, clearly states that multiple vendo r participation should be encouraged. Similar 
condition has also been incorporated in Agreement with Procurement Support Agency (PSA).  
Most of the Purchase is through M/s HLL -HITES (PSA)/GeM. The purchase made outside 
these mode, the tenders are published  in CPPP/Tender wizard portal/Institute website to 
ensure enough /wider participation. However, tender which end up in single bid subject to 
evaluation by the SPC and disposed of accordingly as per the recommendation. 
1.1.10 The stores purchase committee i s also in place to ensure the procurement is done in 
line with extant policy of Government of India (GFR 2017, Manual of procurement of Goods,  
etc). Usually single bids purchases are discouraged.  A procurement proposal passing through 
various committees Su ch as TSEC committee, Tender Committee and CPC before going to 
platform of approval for publishing of document for participation which ensure the proposal 
is aligned with existing public procurement policy of GOI . AIIMS Bhopal has been 
processing tender fo r both national and global enquiry with bid submission period of 
minimum 21 and 28 days respectively, to encourage wider participation from potential 
vendors. Further, technical bid has been opened after ensuring the compliance with Rule 173 
(XX) of the GFR 2017in the event of a single-bidder scenario. 
1.2 STATUS OF FACULTY/NON FACULTY STRENGTH 
 
Recommendations/Observations  
 
1.2.1 The Committee is displeased over the fact that the AIIMS, Bhopal engaged an agency 
for provision of manpower without tendering in violation of General Financial Rules. This 
lapse deprived the Institute from the benefit of the competitive bidding while making 

4 
 
provision for requisite manpower. The Committee, therefore, recommends to the Ministry to 
ensure effective monitoring of the  whole process of tendering in compliance with GFR 
provisions. 
 (Para 2.30 of the report) 
Action Taken 
 
1.2.2 The observation / recommendation of the Hon‘ble Committee have been noted  for 
future guidance. Further, it is submitted that w.e.f. 1 st May 2015 a  new Agency named M/s 
Security Solution and Manpower Services, NOIDA was engaged for providing Manpower 
services, after following due tendering process.  It is also submitted that AIIMS Bhopal 
published e -Tender for ―Hiring of Manpower on outsourcing basis at AIIMS, Bhopal and 
selected M/s Broadcast Engineering Consultants India Limited, New Delhi who is presently 
providing service w.e.f. 1 st January, 2021].  To streamline security system, Now AIIMS 
Bhopal is floating a fresh tender for Manpower Services time  to time at Gem portal in 
accordance with rules and regulations. It is also submitted that AIIMS Bhopal published 
Tender at Gem portal and hire the agency for ―Hiring of Manpower on outsourcing basis at 
AIIMS, Bhopal and selected M/s world class services N ew Delhi who is presently providing 
service w.e.f. 16 June, 2024 to continue.  Additionally, M/s HSCC on nomination basis as per 
Government Guideline and GFR rule 2017 [providing their services from 01-01-2024]. 
 
1.3 SPECIALTY/SUPER SPECIALTY DEPARTMENTS 
 
Recommendations/Observations  
 
1.3.1 The Committee reprimands the AIIMS, Bhopal management over the fact that 
Departments of Rheumatology, TB & Chest, Gastroenterology, Surgical Gastroenterology, 
Urology, Medical Oncology, Clinical Haematology and Neonatology are still not functional at 
the Institute. The Committee is of the view that these are key Departments in providing 
medical aid and in some cases these Departments can provide lifesaving assistance to people. 
The Committee, therefore, recommends to the Institute to start these Specialty and Super 
Specialty Department as early as possible to address sufferings of patient. 
 (Para 2.32 of the report) 
Action Taken 
 
1.3.2 The observation / recommendation of the Hon‘ble Committee have been noted for 
future guidance. All the 26 specialty departments and 16 Super Specialty departments are 
fully functional at AIIMS Bhopal. 
 
1.4 MEDICAL EDUCATION 
Recommendations/Observations  
 
1.4.1 The Committee expects AIIMS, Bhopal to deliver quality medical education in all its  
Departments so that passing out students serve the society in an excellent manner. For this, 

5 
 
basic infrastructure at the Institute is required apart from improved student faculty ratio to 
produce well trained doctors. The Committee also believes that the retention rate of passing 
out students in the Institute would determine the standard of quality medical education being 
imparted in the Institute. 
(Para 2.36 of the report) 
Action Taken 
 
1.4.2 The observation / recommendation of the Hon‘ble Committee have been noted  for 
future guidance.  The MBBS graduates passing out from AIIMS Bhopal are offered Junior 
Residents (Non-academic) post in the Institute. It is submitted that significant percentage of 
students, passing from AIIMS, Bhopal get admissions in MD/MS and DM/M.Ch courses in 
various medical colleges & INIs  of the country including AIIMS, Bhopal and also secure 
admission in Internationally acclaimed Institutions, abroad.  AIIMS Bhopal offers a 
comprehensive range of undergraduate, postgraduate, and doctor al programs, including 
MBBS, MD, MS, DM, M.Ch, and various paramedical courses. Many students passing out 
from AIIMS, Bhopal get admission in MD/MS and DM/M.Ch courses both at AIIMS, Bhopal 
and other INI‘s, and  Medical Colleges. Students also secure admis sion in internationally 
acclaimed Institutions, abroad.  However, the admissions/posts are on competitive basis and 
fill on the basis of the existing vacancies 
 
1.5 STATUS OF BEDS AND WARDS STRENGTH 
 
Recommendations/Observations  
 
1.5.1 The Committee is constrained to express its displeasure that functional bed strength is 
approximately 50 percent of the sanctioned bed strength at the Institute. In Private and 
Ophthalmology wards there are 16 beds out of which only 2 are occupied. It was noted that 
there is no separate ward for Cardiology, Nephrology or Oncology at the Institute.  Given the 
high incidence of number of patients under these categories, the existing facilities are quite 
inadequate. Further, the occupancy rate is less than fifty percent in almost all the wards except 
medical, orthopaedics, surgical, and ICUs. The Committee is concerned to note the abysmally 
low level of bed occupancy for a tertiary care institute of the level of AIIMS. The Committee 
expects AIIMS Bhopal to improve its perception as  a health care provider so that bed 
occupancy/utilization increases substantially. Needless to mention, the Committee is of the 
considered opinion that the merger of BMHRC with AIIMS, Bhopal assumes critical 
importance to ensure optimum utilization of the tertiary healthcare facilities in AIIMS, 
Bhopal. 
(Para 2.47 of the report) 
Action Taken 
 
1.5.2 The observation / recommendation of the Hon‘ble Committee have been noted for 
future guidance. The present status of bed strength / functional wards / ICUs is at tached as 
Annexure –II (l). As of now, the average bed Occupancy at AIIMS Bhopal is 98%.

B. AIIMS RISHIKESH 
 
1.6 Budgetary Allocation 
 
Recommendations/Observations  
 
1.6.1 The Committee is anguished to observe there has been underutilization of funds till 
the year 2016 -17 in AIIMS, Rishikesh. The Committee notes that out of a total budget 
estimate of Rs. 426.50 crores during 2018-19, only Rs. 165 crores of funds have been released 
till 15th October, 2018. The Committee also notes that under GIA (CCA) alloca tion has been 
slashed to Rs 250 crores from the projected demand of Rs 1186.96 crores in BE 2018 -19. The 
Committee is of the opinion that AIIMS, Rishikesh has set up over ambitious  targets for itself 
and therefore, needs to reconsider these demands. A cost  benefit analysis also needs to be 
done vis-à-vis availability of other tertiary care services in the adjoining area. The Committee 
recommends that a common criterion along with a rational approach towards utilization of 
financial allocation for each AIIMS  needs to be done. The Committee is not convinced with 
the justification for overall projected demand to the tune of 1363.46 crores which is far above 
the expenditure incurred in other similar tertiary care center with much higher OPD/IPD load. 
Needless to say the Committee is concerned at the audacious projection which alone is almost 
equal to the sum total of all the remaining five regional AIIMS under study. The Committee 
recommends that suitable benchmark for AIIMS standards be formulated by the Central  
Institute Body (CIB) so that fiscal discipline and prudence in the utilization of the resources is 
done under all circumstances. The Committee recommends that AIIMS, Rishikesh should 
review its projections to a more realistic level. 
(Para 3.9 of the report) 
 
1.6.2 The Committee observes that in -principle approval of many important centers like 
Cardiac Science Center, Neuroscience Center, Ad vance Eye Center, Lung Center  with 
estimated expenditure to the tune of Rs. 100 crores each has been given by the Gov erning 
Body but the construction work could not be started due to unavailability of funds. Besides, 
the Committee is concerned to note that when there has already been a construction of a full -
fledged hospital to seek Rs. 97.61 crore for Administrative blo ck, Rs. 100 crore for the 
academic block, Rs. 100 crore for research block and Rs. 383 crore for residential 
accommodation gives an impression that there is no need for optimum/rational utilization of 
scarce resources. The Committee, therefore, recommends to the Ministry to monitor the 
utilization of funds given to AIIMS, Rishikesh with utmost caution and justification. The 
Committee also recommends for a comparative cost -benefit analysis of AIIMS, Rishikesh 
with other regional AIIMS specifically AIIMS, Bhubaneswar in this regard, as the Committee 
finds it to be the better managed in terms of investment outcome scale and rational utilization 
of limited resources. The Committee is of the opinion that regional AIIMS should provide 
tertiary health care in the n eglected region but should not merely duplicate the facilities 
available with AIIMS Delhi. The Committee recommends that setting up of advanced centers 
should be done only when AIIMS Rishikesh achieves at least 50% of the OPD footfall of 
AIIMS Delhi and th e existing infrastructure is put to optimum usage. Availability of 

7 
 
faculty/Super Specialists to run these advanced centers should also be kept in consideration. 
The Committee, therefore, recommends for consideration of all matters regarding setting up 
of advanced centers by the CIB and also to take into account the budgetary implications if 
these centers are to be setup replicated in all existing and proposed AIIMS. 
(Para 3.10 of the report) 
Action Taken 
 
1.6.3 The observations/recommendation of the Hon‘ble  Committee has been noted  for 
future guidance. In fact, the Ministry was also concerned with the overambitious 
infrastructure expansion plans of AIIMS Rishikesh and had truncated the budget to Rs. 250 
crore and advised AIIMS Rishikesh to prioritize and res trict capital works. The Ministry has 
also put in place a system where all capital expenditure projects costing more than Rs. 100 
crore would come before CIB for consideration. Deliberations before CIB will bring about 
needed policy directions and fiscal d iscipline. It was decided that all major capital investment 
proposals of AIIMS Rishikesh would be reviewed by the Ministry while considering 
financing through the Higher Education Financing Agency (HEFA) and for prioritization of 
investments. HEFA loan san ctioned earlier was subsequently cancelled as the HEFA funding 
was discontinued with effect from September, 2020. Now, the focus has been maintained on 
completion of DPR works and essential works related to consolidation of existing services.  
1.6.4 With r eference to the  observation/recommendation of the Hon'ble Committee, it is 
further stated that AIIMS Rishikesh is projecting budget demand based on a realistic Annual 
Assessment Committee of equipment procurement and Engineering Works. It is further 
ensured that during the Financial Year 2023 -24, the TSA has been implemented and under 
TSA unutilized amount automatically gets lapsed on 31st March. It is further stated that during 
last financial year, the budget/expenditure details are hereunder:

1.6.5 With reference to above, the demand has been projected very realistically and 
accordingly, the utilisation has also improv ed. It is further submitted that during the last 03 
financial years no capital projects above 100 crores has been implemented. With reference to 
the observation/recommendation of the Hon'ble  Committee it is further submitted that DPR 
works of Auditorium, T rauma and Nursing college have been completed. It is further

1.7 STATUS OF CONSTRUCTION WORK 
Recommendations/Observations  
 
1.7.1 The Committee obser ves that there has been a delay in the construction of almost all 
projects and the target date of completion for most of the projects has been further extended. 
The Committee also notes that in most of the cases, delay in the work is due to the non -
performance of construction agency. Such instances have led to delay in construction 
activities implying poor assessment on part of the Institute in selecting the project 
development agency/contractor. The Committee believes that time overruns of 4 -5 years 
cannot be justified on any grounds and this implies poor planning and lackadaisical approach 
on the part of implementing agency. The Committee is of the considered view that project 
contractor with a proven track record of successful and timely completion of wor k should be 
deployed for such important projects. The Committee therefore, recommends that Institute 
must rationally evaluate the projects and plan and chalk out future course of actions, 
accordingly, by prioritizing the projects on the basis of urgency to  avoid delay in completion 
of the projects. 
(Para 3.15 of the report) 
Action Taken 
 
1.7.2 The recommendation of the Hon‘ble Committee has been noted for further guidance. 
It is apprised to the esteemed committee that a system of execution through Executing  
Agency (EA) has been adopted for setting up of new AIIMS and upgradation projects of 
GMCs now. The EAs are fully empowered to take decision on all contractual matters. 
Further, all new AIIMS projects are executed in Engineering, Procurement and Constructi ons 
(EPC) mode in which the construction agency also handles the design matters. In this way the 
coordination issues and consequent delays would be obviated systematically. Construction of 
DPR projects of Trauma Center, Nursing College and Auditorium has b een completed.  
Additionally, at present AIIMS Rishikesh is executing projects under EPC Model for AIIMS 
Satellite Center at Kichha and 150 bedded CCHB at AIIMS Rishikesh. 
 
Recommendations/Observations  
 
1.7.3 During its visit to AIIMS, Rishikesh, the Commi ttee came across a lot of space 
wastage due to structural design faults. The Committee was given to understand that the 
Institute took undue time in correcting the structural deficiencies and thus the development 
work lagged behind. The Committee, therefor e, strongly recommends that the project 
consultants must be held accountable for such serious lapse in the designing and construction 
projects of the Institute. The Committee is alarmed at almost 150 times deviation in the 
original quantities and the actua l quantities as compared to the quantities given in the Bill of 
Quantities (BoQ) of the Contract as pointed by the CAG in its Report. The Committee is of 
the opinion that AIIMS, Rishikesh in itself is a case study of mismanagement, lack of 
foresight and wa stage of public funds. The Committee, therefore, strongly recommends that 

9 
 
the structural/designing faults in AIIMS Rishikesh be corrected to the extent feasible and new 
expansion be done with utmost caution with strict monitoring. 
(Para 3.18 of the report) 
Action Taken 
 
1.7.4 The observations / recommendation of the Hon‘ble Committee have been noted for 
further compliance. A few alterations within the existing hospital building have been made for 
better utilization of the space. Ex -post facto sanction of th e competent authority from the 
central government for the deviations has been taken for most of the projects.

1.9 PROCUREMENT OF EQUIPMENT 
Recommendations/Observations  
 
1.9.1 The Committee, while examining the rationale forwarded by the Institute for the 
procurement of da vinci Robotic Arm is of the opinion that conventional laparoscopic surgery 
takes much lesser time and does not involve usage of expensive consumables. The Committee 
is of the opinion that these resources could have been better utilized in strengthening other 
important ar eas at this nascent stage. The alibi, given regarding training and extended 
warranty as a justification for higher prices, fails to appeal to the Committee, as similar terms 
have been given to other corporate hospitals by the Company across the country. Th e 
Committee is not convinced by these woolly arguments to procure prohibitively expensive 
equipment for a budding Institution like AIIMS. The Committee notes that it has been over 15 
years since the da vinci system (robotic surgery) is being used, but the evidence that it beats 
laparoscopic (minimally invasive surgery) is still lacking. The Committee notes that Robotic 
surgery is several times more expensive than the traditional laparoscopic surgery and engages 
the OT and the surgeon for much longer duratio n. The Committee strongly believes that 
robotic surgery should be used only in centers of excellence like AIIMS Delhi where the 
Institute is in a position to evaluate the efficacy, economics, efficiency and utility of these 
types of surgical intervention a s more research is needed to assess the benefits of robotic 
surgery. The Committee is also concerned that the da vinci system still has monopoly in the 
market and there is every possibility of the company dictating the price and indulging in 
unfair monopol istic practice. The Committee, thus, recommends to the Institute to ensure 
transparency besides appropriate cost/benefit analysis in the procurement of equipment to 
avoid such prohibitively expensive procurement in future. The Committee would like to have 

11 
 
a quarterly report on the usage of the DA Vinci Robotic Arm in both Rishikesh and Jodhpur 
so as to enable it to arrive at a final conclusion in the matter. 
(Para 3.26 of the report) 
Action Taken 
 
1.9.2 The recommendation of the Committee has been noted for further guidance. Quarterly 
Reports on the uses of DA Vinci Robotic Arm in AIIMS, Rishikesh is enclosed at Annexure -
III (c). Till date more than 976+ robotic surgeries have been done in the institute.  
Recommendations/Observations  
 
1.9.3 The Committee is  not satisfied by the reply given by the Institute regarding the 
procurement of equipment on a single bid/proprietary basis as such practices eliminate 
competition resulting in higher procurement costs. The Institute should go for competitive 
tendering to defeat monopolistic tendencies. The Committee, further, feels that the 
notification of tender should get wider reach so that more and more bids are received.  The 
Committee, further, desires a thorough investigation into all single bid and propriety 
purchases. The Committee may be apprised of the conclusions drawn out of the investigation. 
(Para 3.28 of the report) 
Action Taken 
 
1.9.4 The new procurement policy announced by Ministry in March, 2017 clearly states that 
Multiple Vendor Participation should be e ncouraged. Similar condition has also been 
incorporated in Agreement with the Procurement Support Agency (PSA) Procurement. 
Further as per directions of the Parliamentary Standing Committee, if has been decided to 
refer all single bid cases to internal aud it wing of Institute for detailed investigation. AIIMS 
Rishikesh has been advised to send a detailed report in this regard. As per directions of the 
Hon‘ble Parliamentary Standing Committee conveyed by the Ministry of Health and Family 
Welfare vide letter no. H -1103/01/2019-PMSSY-V/EO dated 25 th March, 2019, AIIMS 
Rishikesh was directed to investigate all single bid cases through a Committee. Accordingly, 
a committee consisting of HoDs, F&CAO under the Chairmanship of Medical Superintendent 
investigated all such cases and submitted its report. The Committee concluded that all such 
cases of single tender procurement were done in full compliance of the extant rules of GFR. 
The observation/recommendation of the Hon‘ble Committee has been noted for compliance in 
future. 
Recommendations/Observations  
 
1.9.5 The Committee finds the reply submitted by the Institute as evasive, as the reasons for 
the procurement of equipment with USFDA certification is not submitted. The Committee 
finds that even some of the basic eq uipment have been purchased with USFDA certification. 
The Committee feels that price of equipment with USFDA certification is quite high which 
increases the procurement cost. The Committee feels that the Institute should insist on 
purchasing equipment with  Generic CE/BIS standards so as to promote the "Make in India" 
campaign. The Committee, thus, recommends to the Institute to go for equipment with 

12 
 
CE/BIS standards in future and recommends to the Ministry to issue strict guidelines for the 
procurement of equipment with these standards. 
(Para 3.30 of the report) 
Action Taken 
 
1.9.6 The observation/recommendation of the Hon‘ble Committee has been noted for 
compliance in future. The Ministry issued guidelines  dated 20.02.2018 for allowing of BIS 
standards with other standards like USFDA/CE certification, etc in procurement of medical 
devices equipment which has been circulated by the PMSSY Division of the Ministry of 
HLL/HITES (Procurement Support Agency)/HSCC and all functional AIIMS. PSA has 
intimated that t hey are following the standards of BIS on European CE or US FDA for 
PMSSY procurement and BIS is not an additional requirement for Indian Manufacturer who 
is having any of the certification stated above. Further AIIMS Rishikesh is following the 
Rules, regulations & guidelines of Government of India (GFR-2017) in letter & spirit. 
Recommendations/Observations  
 
1.9.7 The Committee was given to understand that the Institute procured equipment without 
ascertaining the availability of space, manpower and infrast ructure required for installation. 
Due to non-installation of medical equipment, patients were deprived of the benefits. Further, 
operationalization of key diagnostic and in -patient facilities would also have been adversely 
affected. The Committee also fee ls that delay in the installation of equipment may also result 
in lapse of warranty/guarantee of the equipment procured. The Committee recommends to the 
Institute to procure equipment only when there is adequate infrastructure and manpower 
required for the  functioning of the equipment, so that there is no scope for idling or non -
installation of procured equipment. 
(Para 3.32 of the report) 
Action Taken 
 
1.9.8 The observation/recommendation of the Hon‘ble Committee has been noted for future 
guidance. It is s ubmitted that the delays in construction adversely impacted the equipment 
delivery schedule leading to delays in support and installations of equipment. The concerns 
highlighted in the CAG report have been noted. In fact, realizing the problems of delays i n 
installation of equipment, procurement orders have been synchronized with progress of 
construction and progress of manpower recruitment since 2016. The human resource has also 
been engaged in almost all cases. Presently, there are very few cases where th e installation of 
medical equipment is being delayed or the same is lying unused. Presently, there is no 
equipment lying idle or uninstalled for want of space and infrastructure. It is submitted that 
Institute is in the process of installing following equipment's from PSA. 
1. PET CT 
2. PACS 
3. Bone Densitometer (DEXA)

1.10 STATUS OF FACULTY/NON FACULTY STRENGTH 
Recommendations/Observations  
 
1.10.1 The Committee during its study visit to AIIMS, Rishikesh observes that the Institute 
has created a serious regional im balance in the State of Uttarakhand by taking faculty from 
adjoining medical colleges, creating a deficit/vacuum in that area. The idea behind setting up 
of AIIMS in these areas was to create additional capacity and bring the AIIMS, Delhi like 
facilities and services at the tertiary level health care centres in these areas. However, it is 
distressing to note that the Institute has created a serious deficit of medical 
professional/faculty in the entire State of Uttarakhand. The Committee strongly recommends 
that recruitment from adjoining medical colleges in the same state should be done only after 
obtaining NOC from their parent Department. The Committee also recommends that efforts 
should be made to recruit faculty from AIIMS, Delhi so as to create AIIMS li ke culture in the 
regional AIIMS. The Committee is distressed to note that there are hardly any Head of 
Departments/Professors from premier Institutes like AIIMS Delhi/JIPMER/PGI etc. in the 
Institute. Almost 25% of the faculty has been taken with teaching  experience in private 
Institutions and not from any premier Institute. The Committee would also like to have 
detailed information with respect to applicants with adequate teaching experience from large 
tertiary premier Government Institutes/hospitals who have applied for faculty posts till date in 
AIIMS Rishikesh and the reasons for their being not found suitable. 
 (Para 3.39 of the Report) 
Action Taken 
1.10.2 It is submitted that selection of faculty is done by Standing Selection Committee 
followed by app roval of HR Subcommittee/Governing Body.  Applicants from premier 
Government Institutes are also given due weightage. However, in case of non -selection of 
such candidate, reasons of same are not recorded by Standing Selection Committee.  Keeping 
in view the observations, a detailed report with respect to applications with teaching 
experience, etc. is enclosed at Annexure-III (i). The faculty recruitment advertisement is being 
done at national level and wide publicity is given through employment news / Nationa lize 
newspapers/ website. 
 
Recommendations/Observations  
 
1.10.3 The Committee also recommends that faculty from AIIMS, Delhi or equivalent 
Institute be encouraged to impart teaching at AIIMS, Rishikesh as visiting/ guest faculty on a 
regular basis. 
 (Para 3.40 of the Report) 
 
Action Taken 
1.10.4 The recommendation of the Hon‘ble Committee has been noted for future guidance.  
Further, it is submitted that a number of visiting/guest faculty are being invited to AIIMS 
Rishikesh. Visiting faculty / guest faculty is being invited from time to time.

1.11 INTENSIVE CARE UNITS (ICUs) 
 
Recommendations/Observations  
 
1.11.1 The Committee observes that there are only 47 beds, with 18 ventilators and 31 
monitors in ICUs in seven Departments, thereby indicating that the number of monitors and 
ventilators are not commensurate with the number of beds in ICUs if there is 100% 
occupancy at any given point in time. Such inadequate critical care facility at the Institute that 
is a tertiary level care institute indicates a dismal state of affairs with respect to intensive care 
facilities for critical patients. The Committee apprehends that the Institute is no t equipped, 
adequately, to deal with complicated cases that require hospitalization and critical care. The 
Committee therefore, is of the considered view that more intensive care units must be set up 
in various departments to have distinct and specialized medical care facilities. The 
Committee, therefore, strongly recommends that the Institute should expand the scope of 
critical care services to best possible extent with adequate infrastructure and equipment. It is 
also a matter of grave concern to the Comm ittee that the Institute has started a fellowship 
programme in ICU/Critical care without a properly functional ICU. The Committee is of the 
considered view that fellowship programmes are run in Centres of Excellence once they are 
well established so that the standards of these programmes are not lowered. 
 (Para 3.49 of the Report) 
Action Taken 
 
1.11.2 Matter of Record. The observations / recommendation of the Hon‘ble Committee have 
been noted for compliance.  It is submitted that 46 beds will become operati onalized soon  
which will double the current strength of beds. Beds without ventilation are considered as 
Level I ICU beds as they have facilities of short term ventilation by AMBU/ Non Invasive 
Ventilators.  At AIIMS Rishikesh 200 ICU beds with Ventilators  are available.  It is further 
submitted that 150 beds CCHB is under construction.

C. AIIMS JODHPUR 
 
1.13 BUDGETARY ALLOCATION 
 
Recommendations/Observations  
 
1.13.1 The Committee is constrained to express its concern that total 
Observation/Recommendation of the Committee has been noted for future guidance. 
Utilization budgetary allocation in respect  of AIIMS, Jodhpur picked up very slowly. The 
Committee wonders that the percentage utilization was nil in the year 2011 -12, 39.48% in 
2012-13, 47.64% in 2013 -14, 61.56% in 2014 -15, 32.53% in 2015 -16, 50.63% in 2016 -17, 
74.16% in 2017 -18 and 70.09% in 2018 -19 (up to 15.10.2018). The Committee berates that 
Jodhpur management failed to monitor over the implementing agencies during the course of 
execution of construction projects resulting into under -utilization of funds in the initial years 
for Grants-in-Aids (capital). The Committee laments that the trend of expenditure witnessed 
an increase only after the year 2016 -17 that speaks of limited absorption capacity of 
implementation agencies. 
 (Para 4.4 of the Report) 
Action Taken 
 
1.13.2 In the initial phase all  the departments became operational gradually depending upon 
the availability of the faculty, equipment, etc. It is also stated that budget estimate under GIA -
General and Salaries were estimated on higher side with the expectations of recruitment of 
faculty and other staff being in place, higher footfall in IPD and OPD and implementation of 
7th CPC. However, a good number of faculty position remained unfilled due to non -
availability of suitable candidates. Due to this, all the departments could not be 
operationalised as envisaged resulting in underutilization of funds under GIA -Salaries and 
GIA-General. However, more faculty and other supporting staff being on board w.e.f. 2016 -
17 and almost 90% departments being operational by 2017 -18 and implementation of 7th 
CPC for Faculty and Non-faculty, budget utilization position has improved substantially since 
2017-18 as brought out in Para 4.3 above in the later years.  
 
1.13.3 The releases have always been made based on the demand of the respective AIIMS 
only after obtaining the utilization certificates. Larger amount of funds were released to the 
AIIMS to enable them to complete the remaining DPR works and to expand the healthcare 
services. As regards underutilization of funds under GIA -Creation of Capital, it is submitted 
that the details of expenditure as provided in the aforesaid table contains only the expenditure 

17 
 
made towards procurement of medical equipment by AIIMS, Jodhpur. The expenditure on 
construction activities was booked by  MoHFW. It was only in the y ear 2015 -16 that all 
payment related to RA Bills of construction activities was transferred to AIIMS, Jodhpur vide 
MoHFW letter No. Z. 28016/267/2 014-SSH dated 31.08.2015. Thus, depicting gradual 
increase in the budget utilization over the years. The Insti tute and Ministry shall take 
necessary action for optimum utilization of funds and shall also ensure effective financial 
monitoring mechanism in future.  Effective financial monitoring and optimum utilization of 
funds are now being ensured as would be seen from the updated status given in Para 4.3. It is 
also evident that total utilization of the funds during the financial years 2019 -20 and 2020-21 
is up to 90.91% and 92.49% respectively. Therefore, the Institute has ensured optimum 
utilization of the funds as per GFR norms. 
 
Recommendations/Observations  
 
1.13.4 The Committee was given to understand by the Ministry/Institute that the funds 
received were sufficient to meet the construction activities and on-going development projects 
at the Institute. The Com mittee believes that the problematic aspects relate to timely 
execution of the projects. The Committee, therefore, arrives at the conclusion that had the 
implementing agencies executed all segments of the projects in time, with required absorption 
capacity, there would have been optimum utilization of allocated funds and lesser delays. The 
Committee is of the considered view that had the Institute and the Ministry undertaken proper 
planning and periodic monitoring of the project work there would have been o ptimal and 
judicious utilization of resources. The Committee, therefore, recommends to the Ministry to 
institute an enquiry to assess the actual reasons for under -utilization of allocated funds for 
execution of construction projects and accordingly apprise  the Committee of the action taken 
against the implementing agencies as per terms and conditions of the contract. 
 (Para 4.5 of the Report) 
Action Taken 
 
1.13.5 It is submitted that the major reasons for delay in Project is due to delay in 
construction activities happened on account of systematic issues. These delays are attributed 
due to delay in release of GFC drawings and delay in approval of variation, work on hold in 
want of non -finalization of MGPS, Modular OT, CSSD, Kitchen equipment & delay on 
approval due to change in layout or original plans of D&T OT 2nd to 6th Floor. The matter 
has been resolved and  at present the work of modular OT is in progress by M/s MDD & 
accordingly civil work is also in progress as per revised drawing by M/s L&T. Institute  shall 
also ensure proper planning and periodic monitoring with coordination of implementing 
agencies leading to optimum and judicious utilization of funds. All efforts are being made to 
complete the remaining work, at the earliest. 
 
Recommendations/Observations  
 
1.13.6 The Committee is not convinced with the reasons given for underutilization of funds 
as these routine activities are often faced in the functioning of any institution. The delay in the 

18 
 
completion of projects has an adverse impact on the health care services to be provided by the 
Institute. The Committee expects the Department to uphold the principles of fiscal discipline 
and sound financial management by judiciously utilizing the remaining funds. Needless to 
say, that only proper planning, co -ordination with implementing agencies and suitable 
monitoring mechanism could have ensured optimum utilization of funds. 
 (Para 4.8 of the Report) 
Action Taken 
 
1.13.7 The observations / recommendation of the Hon‘ble Committee has been noted  for 
future guidance. With proper planning, coordination with implementing agencies and close 
monitoring, it has been possible to substantially improve the fund utilization position in 2018 -
19 as brought out in Para 4.3 above. Attempts have been made by Ministry to furt her fine-
tune the releases using PFMS so as to maintain a fund balance of about 03 months 
requirement. The Institute and Ministry shall take necessary action for optimum utilization of 
funds and shall also ensure effective financial monitoring mechanism in future. 
 
Recommendations/Observations  
 
1.13.8 The Committee opines that diversion of funds is a result of lack of effective financial 
control and monitoring of actual utilization. Such diversion amounts to misappropriation of 
allocated fund for the intended purpose. The Committee, thus, recommends framing of proper 
guidelines to control such diversion. The Committee, in this connection, strongly recommends 
for strengthening of financial management, effective monitoring mechanism and adoption of 
proper accounting procedure for an effective internal financial control. 
 (Para 4.10 of the Report) 
Action Taken 
 
1.13.9 The observations / recommendation of the Hon‘ble Committee have been noted 
carefully and Institute would ensure due care for utilization of GIA f or the intended purpose 
only and avoid diversion of funds from one head to another by exercising  effective financial 
control and budget monitoring.  It is however stated here that for aforesaid diversion noticed 
by the Hon‘ble Committee happened in earlier year due to the reason that sufficient funds 
under the particular GIA Head were not provisioned as in the budget and diversion had to be 
resorted to meet urgent fund requirements. 
 
1.14 STATUS OF CONSTRUCTION WORK 
 
Recommendations/Observations  
 
1.14.1 The Committee expresses its anguish to observe faulty drawings in the construction 
project and subsequent shifting of OT from 2nd floor to 6th floor in AIIMS, Jodhpur as the 
same constituted the reasons for delay in the completion of construction projects. Th e 
Committee wonders that the faulty design in the original drawing was not taken seriously at 
the initial stage. The Committee, therefore, recommends that accountability for such faulty 

19 
 
design should be fixed and a detailed report on the reasons for the de lay in the 
operationalization of the OTS be submitted to the Committee for its consideration. The 
Committee also recommends that the remaining work of package II be completed within the 
specified deadline. The Ministry in tandem with institute management m ust oversee and 
ensure timely execution of all projects. 
 (Para 4.13 of the Report) 
Action Taken 
 
1.14.2 The observations / recommendation of the Hon‘ble Committee has been noted  for 
future guidance. The Institute is working to ensure that remaining work to be completed at the 
earliest possible without further delay.  It is submitted that the Operation Theatres (MOT) 
were not shifted from 2nd to 6th floor. The Operation Theatres at AIIMS Jodhpur are from 
2nd to 6th floor and further only internal changes in drawing were made keeping the 
functionality in mind. As the drawings of the construction project were completed well before 
the tender of modular operation theatres were envisaged/planned at All India Institute of 
Medical Sciences (AIIMS), Jodhpur. The bas ic modular operation theatre designs and the 
tender requirement for Modular operation theatres necessitated the following changes: - 
 
1. Provision for Dedicated Air Handling Units (AHU‘S) for individual operation theatres 
is a mandatory part of Modular ope ration theatre with each modular theatre requiring a fine 
control of both the temperature and humidity as per the revised guidelines for air conditioning 
in operation theatres issued by NABH (Extract of relevant page of Guidelines is enclosed for 
reference). This was not provided for in the original drawings and needed to be added as per 
the requirements for modular operation theatres. 
 
2. Setting up of Modular operation theatre also required the creation of Uninterrupted 
Power Supply (UPS) for each operati on theatre complex to ensure continuous trouble free 
service. This was not provided in the original drawings and needed to be added as per the 
requirement for modular operation theatres. 
 
3. There is also a requirement for an internal passage/connectivity between the Intensive 
Care Units and the operation theatres to allow for movement of sick patients between these 
two interdependent and critical areas of the hospital. The connectivity was not provided for in 
the original drawings necessitating modifications. 
 
4. Due to the above mentioned mandatory provisions for AHU, UPS and ICU 
connectivity, a partial rework of the floor plan and drawings was extremely vital and 
indispensable. Only the vital and necessary changes were incorporated. 
 
1.14.3 At present, al l 30 Nos Modular OTs have been completed in all respect and are fully 
functional. The works awarded to all the 04 packages of AIIMS Jodhpur have already been 
completed. 
 
 
 
 

20 
 
Recommendations/Observations  
 
1.14.4 The Committee would like to draw the attentio n of the Institute to the recent 
observation by the CAG in context of AIIMS Jodhpur, where it has mentioned about price 
escalation in violation of contract, repeated grant of time extension, release of payment 
despite poor workmanship, excess release of mo bilization advance and avoidable/extra 
contractual payments. The Committee is also of the view that replies of the Ministry are not at 
all acceptable and the difference between the original and the final cost cannot be considered 
beyond the purview of cost  escalation. From the information provided by the Ministry, it is 
evident that there has been cost overrun of Rs. 9.00 crore under package I and Rs. 18.00 crore 
under package II. Even if the cost of construction of these projects did not increase during th e 
staggered period of six years, it implies that cost evaluation/calculation was inflated to such a 
great extent in the initial stage itself. The Committee wonders as to how the original cost -
estimation of the projects was done. The Committee, therefore, s eeks reasons/explanation 
over the cost-estimation of the projects in all the four packages. 
 (Para 4.15 of the Report) 
Action Taken 
 
1.14.5 It is submitted that the work under package I and Package II AIIMS Jodhpur had been 
executed as per Good for Constru ction (GFC) drawings issued by the Design DPR. The 
increase in completion cost of Package-I is due to variations. These variations occurred due to 
erroneous BOQ and difference in BOQ & GFC drawings. There was no escalation clause 
existed in package I contr act agreement and hence accordingly no payment toward escalation 
had been allowed.  The sanction for package II was based on tendered BOQ cost only, the 
escalation to be paid as per in built clauses of contract was left out from the sanctioned cost, 
may be due to an oversight. The escalation had been allowed in package II as per contract 
agreement only after Financial Advisor AIIMS Jodhpur scrutiny & concurrence.  There has 
been saving in package III & package IV completion cost. The total project cost of all the four 
packages together is within permissible limits of 10%. 
 
1.14.6 The works were in progress so provisional extensions were granted to keep the 
contract alive without prejudice to the right of the government to levy compensation in 
accordance with the provisions under clause 2 of contract agreement and also as per provision 
of CPWD Works Manual.  The works had been executed as per approved drawings, 
specifications and contract agreement. The original cost estimation of all the four packages 
had been p repared by Design DPR consultant through in house consultant M/s HLL.  As has 
been apprised to the CAG, the additional mobilization advance was paid to the respective 
contractors due to financial crunch being faced by them. Several firms in the civil 
construction business were facing liquidity crunch during that period. 
 
1.14.7 It would be relevant to bring out here that the Government charged market rate interest 
from the contractors and hence the advance may not be viewed as a financial assistance. 
Further, the mobilisation advance including first mobilisation advance was sanctioned to 
contractors against a bank guarantee. The advances were recovered in full along with interest. 
It would also be a relevant consideration that this intervention did actually h elp in completion 

21 
 
of the Medical college package in the respective Institutes. The Ministry might have landed 
up with contractual complications and incomplete works as has actually happened with 
respect to many other packages, if the Ministry had not suppo rted the contractors facing the 
financial crunch. 
 
Recommendations/Observations  
 
1.14.8 The Committee, further, observes that under package III and package IV, there has 
been time overrun of approximately three years. The Committee feels that the delayed 
execution of the projects in turn, has severely hampered the healthcare services to the patients 
during this period. Such delays have impaired the capacity of the Institute to provide the 
envisaged levels of treatment, health care and medical education. Th e Committee desires the 
Ministry to assess the genuine reasons for delay in the execution of the projects under 
construction and apprise the Committee in this regard. The Ministry must conduct detailed 
site investigation, hold regular meetings and formulat e effective monitoring mechanism to 
ensure that the project is managed efficiently after meticulous scheduling of work. 
 (Para 4.17 of the Report) 
Action Taken 
 
1.14.9 The observations / recommendation of the Hon‘ble Committee has been noted for 
future guidance. The delay in completion of Package -III and Package-IV were mainly due to 
non – availability of work front from Package -I & Package -II. However, the institute 
functioning was not hampered as the required engineering services under package III & IV 
were made available in the completed physical infrastructure from 2012 onwards. As of now, 
package – III & package IV works have been completed as brought out in the following table:

1.15 Procurement of Medical Equipment 
 
Recommendations/Observations  
 
1.15.1 The Committee appreciates the role played by the Assessment Committee in 
procurement of medical equipment. However, the Committee is constrained to note that no 
separate analysis has been done for the procurement of equipment, thus indicating  that the 
equipment have been purchased without analysing the cost of the equipment vis -à-vis its 
actual utility. The Committee is of the view that procurement of equipment without proper 
cost-benefit analysis may result in underutilization/non -utilization/ duplication of equipment 
and procurement of equipment at higher prices. The Committee, therefore, recommends to the 
Institute/Ministry that proper cost -benefit analysis must be done by the Assessment 
Committee before the procurement of the equipment so t hat the utility of the equipment is 
explicit commensuration to its cost leading of effective delivery to health services. This 
analysis should form the part of the procurement process. The Committee also reiterates its 
observation made in context of AIIMS,  Rishikesh (para 3.26) for the procurement of da -vinci 
robotic arm. The Committee also recommends that the Assessment Committee must go into 
the details of reasons for proprietary/ single bid purchases and submit a status Report to the 
Committee along with a quarterly update on the usage of da-vinci robotic arm. 
 (Para 4.22 of the Report) 
Action Taken 
 
1.15.2 The observations / recommendation of the Hon‘ble Committee has been noted for  
future guidance. As the institute is being established, the procurement of medical equipment 
is being done for the first time and is in line with usual equipment available in similar 
departments across other similar INIs. No formal cost -benefit analysis has been done for 
procurement of medical equipment. However, whenever depa rtment requests for an 
equipment, it does an internal exercise regarding its requirement / essentiality patient care. 
The assessment committee, then thoroughly evaluates the cost justification, the proposed 

23 
 
benefit for healthcare and a proper justification  for expenditure over other competing 
requirements of other departments. It is only after a thorough deliberation & discussion that 
the assessment committee approves the purchase of each and every equipment.  However, the 
institute wishes to put on record t hat the other AIIMS were established with a mandate to 
establish a centre of excellence in healthcare and to reduce the workload on AIIMS Delhi. 
The recommendation that high -end equipment should only be limited to AIIMS Delhi goes 
against the mandate of th e new AIIMS and is likely to be counterproductive by stymieing the 
future growth of the institution. Till date 104 robotic surgeries have been done in the Institute. 
 
Recommendations/Observations  
 
1.15.3 The Committee is anguished to observe the delay in installation of equipment on the 
account of procrastination in the construction of new OTs. This is a peculiar situation when 
equipment has been purchased without ascertaining a place for its installation. The Committee 
believes that delay in installation of equipment may lead to expiry of guarantee/warranty of 
the equipment, service period. The Committee, thus, recommends that equipment should be 
procured only when space for their installation is ready so as to avoid undue delay in the 
installation and allay the fear of expiry of guarantee/warrantee of the equipment. Not just this, 
equipment should not be allowed to remain idle / unutilized. The Committee, further, 
recommends that the Ministry should expedite the process of construction of new OTs 
immediately so that the installation of procured equipment is ensured at the earliest. 
 (Para 4.24 of the Report) 
Action Taken 
 
1.15.4 The observations / recommendation of the Hon‘ble Committee have been noted for  
future guidance. Anaesthesia work stations pending installation (HLL/PCD/PMSSY/AIIMS-
II/03/14-15/7344) were received at AIIMS Jodhpur in January 2018 and OT table for CTVS 
department (HLL/PCD/PMSSY/AIIMS -II/21/15-16/6428) in March 2016. The procurement 
process for the above medical equipment was done by M/ s HLL Life Care Ltd. based on the 
directions of the Ministry of Health and Family Welfare for all the new AIIMS including 
AIIMS, Jodhpur way back in 2014 -2015 and 2015 -2016 respectively. AIIMS, Jodhpur 
ensures that the procured instruments are installed pr omptly and in time as soon as the 
site/user department is ready for installation. 
 
1.15.5 It is also stated that the delays in construction also adversely impacted the equipment 
delivery schedule leading to delays in supply and installations of equipment's . The concerns 
highlighted in the CAG report have been noted. In fact, realizing the problems of delays in 
installation of equipment's, since 2016 the procurement orders have been synchronized with 
progress of construction and progress of manpower recruitm ent. The human resource has also 
been appointed in almost all cases. Presently, there are hardly any cases where the installation 
of medical equipment's is being delayed or the same is lying unused.  Guarantee/ warranty and 
service period usually starts fro m the date of installation of machines/ medical equipment. 
Hence, it will not affect the guarantee/ warranty period. AIIMS, Jodhpur is committed to early 
installation of procured equipment and the same will be done as soon as the new OTs is ready 

24 
 
for use.  As regards, Procurement done by AIIMS Jodhpur, it is informed that the institute 
procured the equipment only when the required expertise, adequate space and patient load 
was there. Both the items have been installed, Details are as below:

Recommendations/Observations  
 
1.15.6 The Committee understands that AIIMS, Jodhpur is meant for t he needy and poor 
patients requiring tertiary health care facilities  where quality of equipment can be lifesaving 
for the patients. The Committee, however, observes that since basic equipment like OT Light 
System, Laparoscopic Set, Suction Machine, Multipa ra Monitor and many others are easily 
available with European and also with Indian manufacturers. The procurement of these 
medical equipment with USFDA certification should have been resorted to only in the rarest 
of circumstances, after ensuring, in writi ng, that no alternative with CE/BIS certification is 
available. The Committee desires to be apprised of the reasons for procurement of the above 
mentioned devices with USFDA certification when equivalent quality and lower price 
CE/BIS certified equipment were available. 
(Para 4.29 of the Report) 
 
1.15.7 The Committee is of the considered view that procurement of equipment with BIS 
certification will encourage companies to manufacture their products in India and also 
increase their investment. This will also  contribute to ―Make in India‖, programme of the 
Government of India to promote domestic manufacturers. The Committee, therefore, 
recommends to the Ministry to issue strict directions to all AIIMS like institutions to procure 
equipment with wider certification standards so as to promote competitive pricing and also to 
encourage ―Make in India‖ scheme. 
 (Para 4.30 of the Report) 
Action Taken 
 
1.15.8 It has been submitted by AIIMS Jodhpur that the OT Lights, Laparoscopic sets, 
suction machines for operation t heatres, multipara monitors etc are high end equipment and 
are critical components towards providing quality care especially in Intensive Care Units, 
Operation Theatres and for emergency services. Especially, All India Institute of Medical 

25 
 
Sciences, Jodhpur, being an apex tertiary care Institution in the country, has the mandate to 
provide the best healthcare to the needy population being served by it and never attempts to 
dilute the patient care being provided to them by procuring substandard equipment. Regarding 
the use of USFDA, the technical committee evaluated the requirement for quality 
certifications and used USFDA only in following circumstances –  
 
1.  Required circumstances on advice of technical committee. 
 
2. The committee is of the opinion that optimal quality of medical equipment will be diluted 
in case of utilizing any other quality certification. 
 
 3. Or in cases where adequate indigenous medical device certifications have not been laid 
down for a particular medical equipment in India. 
 
1.15.9 It may also be mentioned here that the Ministry vide OM dated 20.02.2018 has issued 
guidelines for adopting the BIS standards also as an option to bidders, wherever available, and 
since then procurement is being made accordingly. The Ministry has already initiated action 
in this regard. Secretary, Ministry of Health and Family Welfare vide letter dated 7 th May, 
2018 to Director General, Bureau of Indian Standards requested formulation of specifications 
for all the commonly procured medical equipment to pro mote ‗Make in India‘ policy of the 
Government of India. Bureau of Indian Standards has already started creation of BIS 
standards for Medical Equipment and Devices. As per information available with the Ministry 
around standardization list for around 1000 medical devices have almost been completed.

1.17.3 AIIMS Jodhpur takes its responsibility for training PG students very seriously, 
offering a well - structured program across preclinical, preclinical, and clinical department s. 
This broad-based and flexible approach ensures that students receive the essential education 
needed to meet the healthcare demands of our country. PG students at AIIMS Jodhpur receive 
training that is adapted to the evolving needs of society, equipping them to effectively assume 
the responsibilities of various service roles in healthcare.  In line with its mandate, AIIMS 
Jodhpur adopts a holistic approach to PG student training, upholding  the highest standards of 
teaching, research, and patient care. Our goal is to ensure that our institution ranks alongside 
other Institutes of National Importance, such as AIIMS  Delhi, PGI, and JIPMER, and we 
continuously strive to achieve this. 
 
Recommendations/Observations  
 
1.17.4 The Committee apprehends that inadequat e faculty as well as limited clinical training 
could be one of the reasons for PG seats not being filled up in the year 2018. The Committee 
believes that exposure of students to proper clinical training would help enriching their skills 
and experience in m edical education. The Committee, therefore, expects the Institute to 
improve the level of its faculty as well as clinical standards and ensure that from next session, 
henceforth, that all the PG seats are filled up. The Committee also recommends to the 
Institute to start the newly approved PG courses after assessing availability of adequate 
faculty and requisite patient load. The Committee has been apprised of a abysmally low level 
of clinical exposure to PG students in AIIMS, Jodhpur specifically MD (Anaes thesiology). 
The Committee recommends that a quarterly status note be submitted to the Committee on the 
clinical exposure of PG Students (discipline -wise) so as to ensure that educational standards 
are not diluted. 
 (Para 4.46 of the Report) 

29 
 
Action Taken 
 
1.17.5 AIIMS Jodhpur has made significant strides in its postgraduate (PG) medical 
education program, demonstrating a strong commitment to providing high -quality education 
and training to its students. For the July 2024 session, all 121 sanctioned seats fo r PG courses 
(MD/MS/MDS/DM/MCh) were filled following the counselling process. For the current 
January 2025 session, 133 PG seats were advertised, and as of now, 84 seats have been filled, 
with the remaining seats still being allocated through ongoing coun selling. In addition to 
expanding the number of seats in various broad and super specialty courses, AIIMS Jodhpur 
has also introduced new PG courses, including Post -doctoral fellowship courses, which have 
been well-received by students pursuing them with e nthusiasm. AIIMS Jodhpur is committed 
to providing PG students with a well -rounded education, offering a comprehensive and 
structured program that spans preclinical, preclinical, and clinical departments. This broad -
based, flexible approach equips students  with the essential skills and knowledge required to 
meet the healthcare demands of the country. The institution places great emphasis on training 
that aligns with society's evolving healthcare needs, ensuring students are well -prepared for 
roles in health care service delivery. Students at AIIMS Jodhpur gain extensive clinical 
exposure through exposure to a wide range of cases and diseases that they encounter during 
their time at the institute. 
 
1.17.6 With a high patient volume across both outpatient and e mergency services, AIIMS 
Jodhpur offers PG students a robust clinical and academic training experience. The hospital 
sees approximately 5,000 patients daily in the OPD, with around 250 patients admitted to 
trauma and emergency care each day. The institutio n typically admits over 200 patients daily 
in the inpatient department (IPD) and performs an average of 33 major surgeries each day, 
totalling nearly 18,500 major surgeries in the past year. AIIMS Jodhpur also manages 140 
ICU beds dedicated to the care of critically ill patients and managed by the Department of 
Anaesthesia and Critical Care. These high levels of clinical activity ensure that PG students, 
especially MD (Anaesthesiology), receive substantial hands -on experience, allowing them to 
develop the skills needed for real-world medical practice. In addition to clinical exposure, the 
PG Orientation Program at AIIMS Jodhpur spans one and a half months and aims to offer a 
holistic learning experience. Students participate in training sessions on research 
methodology, biostatistics, and the integration of artificial intelligence (AI) in medicine. The 
institution also hosts a PG Inaugural Ceremony for newly joined residents, which includes 
motivational guest speakers to inspire and guide the students as they  embark on their 
postgraduate journey. 
 
1.17.7 The recruitment of faculty at AIIMS Jodhpur is an ongoing process, with a focus on 
maintaining the highest standards of quality. As of now, the institution employs 220 medical 
faculty members and 43 nursing fa culty members. However, some senior faculty positions 
remain vacant in certain clinical departments, due to a lack of applicants. Despite these 
challenges, AIIMS Jodhpur assures the committee that it continues to work diligently to fill 
these faculty posit ions and improve its academic infrastructure.  AIIMS Jodhpur continues to 
be a leading institution for postgraduate medical education, providing students with a 
dynamic and well -rounded experience that prepares them for the challenges of medical 

30 
 
practice. The institution remains committed to its goals of enhancing the quality and scope of 
medical education and training, ensuring the best outcomes for its students and the healthcare 
system as a whole. 
 
Recommendations/Observations  
 
1.17.8 The Committee opine s that an effective and relevant training module expands the 
capacity building of the medical and nursing students and it therefore, plays an important role 
in their career development. Clinical trainings help the healthcare professionals to better 
understand the subject and prepare them for the challenges they will face in their career. The 
Committee, therefore, recommends that the Institute must start clinical training programmes 
for the students and ensure that there is adequate faculty at the Institute to support these 
programmes. 
 (Para 4.47 of the Report) 
Action Taken 
 
1.17.9 AIIMS Jodhpur is well -equipped to offer a comprehensive and robust clinical and 
academic training experience for its students. With the high patient volume, both in OPD and 
emergency services, along with significant numbers of major surgeries and ICU beds, the 
clinical exposure for Postgraduate students seems extensive. Approximately 5,000 patients 
visit the OPD daily, with around 250 patients presenting in the trauma and emergency  
departments each day. The daily IPD admissions typically exceed 200 patients, and an 
average of 33 major surgical interventions are carried out each day. In the past year, nearly 
18,500 major surgeries were performed. Additionally, we have 140 ICU beds de dicated to 
managing critically ill patients. The infrastructure and laboratory facilities are state of the art 
for training PG students in all aspects and crucial in helping students build a strong 
foundation. This kind of exposure is vital in ensuring tha t students are well -prepared for the 
challenges of medical practice. 
 
1.17.10 The structured programs like the "Preparation for Interns" (PRINT) program 
and PG Orientation Program for one -and-a-half-month period, also demonstrate a clear effort 
to provide a holistic learning experience, including aspects like research methodology, 
biostatistics, and AI integration. Additionally, we organize a PG Inaugural Ceremony for 
newly joined residents, featuring a guest speaker to motivate and inspire our Post graduat e 
students. Faculty recruitment at AIIMS Jodhpur is progressing at a fast pace, with regular 
hiring processes that maintain a strong focus on the quality of selections. However, despite 
repeated efforts, senior faculty positions in certain clinical departments remain vacant due to a 
lack of response from applicants. Nonetheless, AIIMS Jodhpur assures the Hon‘ble 
Committee that every possible effort is being made to fill these vacancies, and the Institute 
will continue to work diligently to address this issue. 
 
 
 

31 
 
1.18 Status of Operation Theatres 
Recommendations/Observations  
 
1.18.1 The Committee is dismayed to note that 3 major and 2 minor OTs in Trauma OT 
complex do not have medical gas pipe line at present and the supply of gas is ensured by 
using Cylinders. The Committee is of the view that MGPS is a life -supporting element crucial 
to life of a patient. The Committee, therefore, recommends to the Ministry to ensure that all 
the OTs are equipped with MGPS and make them fully equipped with the latest equipm ent 
without any delay especially in trauma complex so as to cater to the critical patients. 
 (Para 4.49 of the Report) 
Action Taken 
 
1.18.2 The Medical gas pipeline system has been installed in trauma OT complex and it has 
been made functional. 
Recommendations/Observations  
 
1.18.3 The Committee during the course of its study visit to Jodhpur found that not a single 
modular OT was functioning. The 9 major OTs and 3 Minor OTs were make shift OTs in the 
OPD blocks and not were in consonance with the original drawings submitted by the DDPRC 
which had been duly approved. These OT designs were arbitrarily changed on the pretext that 
there were designing faults. The Committee has been further apprised that there was no 
provision for AHU in the original drawing. Th e Committee is constrained to observe that the 
matter was not taken up with the DDPRC and nor was it penalized or its contract terminated. 
The Committee would like to be apprised of the action taken by the Ministry against the 
DDPRC and also whether the Mi nistry had provided for the provision of Modular OTs in the 
initial design itself. The Committee is of the opinion that this negligence has led to severe 
delays with great financial implications in the form of arbitration claims submitted by the 
contractor L&T in this case besides creating structural stability concerns. The Committee 
strongly recommends for a thorough probe in the entire matter including fixing responsibility. 
The Committee would also like to be apprised whether this has resulted in any cos t/time 
overruns besides creating grounds for arbitration and submit the same to the Committee 
within three months of submission of this report. 
 (Para 4.52 of the Report) 
Action Taken 
 
1.18.4 The work of modular OT was delayed in want of tender finalizatio n from HLL. 
However, on arrival of modular OT agency M/s. MDD at site, space/site were made available 
to them and accordingly necessary changes in civil work. Presently 34 Modular OTs are 
functional at AIIMS, Jodhpur. 
 
 
 

32 
 
Recommendations/Observations  
 
1.18.5 The Committee also recommends AIIMS Jodhpur to expedite the construction of OTs 
in a time bound manner and reduce the waiting time period for patients awaiting surgical 
procedures thereby reducing their distress and out of pocket expenditure. The Commit tee 
would also like the Institute to submit a quarterly report on the number of procedures done per 
OT per day in order to gauge the optimum utilization of the functional OTs. 
 (Para 4.54 of the Report) 
Action Taken 
 
1.18.6 The observations / recommendatio n of the Hon‘ble Committee has been noted  for 
future guidance.  The scheduled operation theatre timings of the functional OTs are from 8 
A.M. to 5.00 P.M. However, the OT timings regularly over  shoots the scheduled time. The 
emergency operation theatre is b eing run round the clock.  Regarding the number of 
procedures per OT, it is pertinent to point out that number of cases being done in an operation 
theatre is dependent on the nature and complexity of cases with the more complex cases 
taking a much longer ti me. However, it is informed that total 9687 surgical procedures were 
performed at AIIMS Jodhpur in 2018.
