1.19 Status of ICUs, Trauma & Emergency Services and Wards 
Recommendations/Observations  
 
1.19.1 The Committee observes that availability of 17 ventilators against 19 beds would not 
cater in case of 100 percent occupancy at any given time. Even 100 percent bed occupancy 
with mere 19 beds in the ICUs in normal circumstances wou ld not cater to emergency 
situations because all units would be fully occupied. The Committee recommends that the 

33 
 
number of bed in ICU be increased to accommodate all critically ill patients visiting the 
hospital. The Committee also notes that emergency services have not yet started on 24x7x365 
days basis and only referred cases are being taken inspite of the fact that the trauma building 
was completed in 2014 -15. The Committee however appreciates the integrated approach of 
the Institute to have a synergy with the Indian army so as to cater to emergency situations and 
recommends the institute to start 24 X 7 X 365 days' trauma/emergency care. 
 (Para 4.59 of the Report) 
Action Taken 
 
1.19.2 Intensive Care Unit (ICU ) is fully functional and occupied  with 160 b eds. Trauma & 
Emergency is operational 24*7*365 and catering both adult and pediatric patients. The 
Trauma & Emergency Department has experienced a 72% increase in patient load, from 
25,240 in 2019 to 89,707 in 2024. To address this surge, a 12 -bed Trauma High-Dependency 
Unit (HDU) was established in June 2024, providing critical intensive care for major trauma 
cases and significantly improving patient outcomes. As a result, patient referrals have been 
reduced to negligible levels since 2024. Additionally, the Paediatric Emergency Unit is also 
efficiently managing all complex paediatric emergency referrals, ensuring comprehensive 
care for critically ill children. 
Recommendations/Observations  
 
1.19.3 The Committee finds the figure of 684 functional beds, 70%  of the total sanctioned 
bed strength (960) in 20 different wards functional at the Institute. The lower number of 
functional beds and even further lower level of bed occupancy reflects poor perception of 
healthcare services being provided by the Institute . The Committee is concerned that existing 
beds are insufficient to cater to the growing health needs of the people. Such inadequacies 
have forced people across socio -economic strata to unaffordable private healthcare facilities. 
The Institute needs to urg ently increase the bed occupancy by improving its perception to 
address the health services for the people of the region. 
 (Para 4.61 of the Report) 
Action Taken 
 
1.19.4 The sanctioned bed strength of AIIMS Jodhpur is 960 beds. However, the institute is 
operating with 1046 functional beds to cater to the needs of the patients. The present bed 
occupancy is more than 90%. IPD  admissions have increased to 60% from 31212 in 2019 to 
78257 in 2024 with significant rises observed in all the departments. 
1.20 Governing Body 
Recommendations/Observations  
 
1.20.1 The Committee during its deliberations and visit to AIIMS, Jodhpur found that the 
meetings of the Governing Body at the Institute were not being held on a regular basis. The 
Members of the Governing Body are not consulted while taking decisions during the meeting 
and all the decisions are taken by few incumbents. The Committee is of the firm view that the 

34 
 
meetings of the Governing Body at the Institute should be conducted on time and 
participation of all membe rs of the body should be ensured while taking important decisions. 
All AIIMS like institutions should strive to conduct regular meetings and participation of all 
members be encouraged while taking important decisions of the Institute. The Committee, 
accordingly, recommends that AIIMS, Jodhpur must give due importance to all the Members 
of the Governing Body while taking any important decision to make it democratically 
decentralized. All out efforts must be made for strengthening of Governing Body and other 
Committees for better functioning of AIIMS, Jodhpur. 
(Para 4.68 of the Report) 
 
1.20.2 The Committee is of the considered opinion that autonomy and accountability need to 
be balanced. The Committee welcomes the appointment of the Presidents of the Institut e and 
the constitution of the CIB at the Central level. This will ensure a sense of responsibility in 
the decision making process. 
 (Para 4.69 of the Report) 
Action Taken 
 
1.20.3 The observation/ recommendation of committee has been noted for future guidan ce. 
The Central Government had constituted the Institute Body through vide notification dated 
18th May, 2020. Subsequently, Governing Body and other important committees were 
constituted through vide Order No. 2363 dated 31/08/2020 that facilitated holding  of Institute 
Body and Governing Body meetings on regular basis, whenever required.  It is further noted 
that 09th Meeting of the Governing Body of the Institute was held on 07th November, 2024.

1.21 BUDGETARY ALLOCATION 
 
Recommendations/Observations  
 
1.21.1 The Committee expresses its deep concern that the percentage of underutilisation with 
respect to total Grants-in-aid of AIIMS, Patna has been 64.72% in 2012 -13, 37.98% in 2013 -
14, 47.15% in 2014-15, 32.33% in 2015-16, 42.86% in 2016-17 and 62.61% in 2017-18. The 
closing balance was as high as Rs. 195.95 crore for the year 2015 -16 and 208.70 crore for the 
year 2016-17 that express low absorption capacity of implementing agencies. The Committee 
also observes that in the year 2014 -15, the percentage of utilization under GiA (capital) was 
18.73% which further decreased to 12.93% in 2015 -16. In 2016 -17, the opening balance 
under capital grants in aids was 140.73 crore implying that out of the total 161.63 crore 
allotted under GiA (Capital) on ly Rs 20.90 crore were spent in capital formation in 2015 -16. 
All these date speak volume of poor financial planning estimation and control execution of 
construction scheme. 
(Para 5.4 of the Report) 
 

35 
 
1.21.2 The Committee laments the fact that AIIMS Patna t hat is envisaged as an apex 
institute of medical learning and affordable tertiary health care has failed miserably in 
optimum utilization of its budget. The budgetary expenditure pattern of AIIMS Patna exposes 
the lackadaisical attitude of the Institute an d brings to the foreground the Institute‘s 
incompetency to use the financial resources to its fullest potential. This gross underutilisation 
of resources is a grave matter of concern especially when AIIMS Patna lags behind on many 
fronts and struggles to provide quality healthcare to the citizens. 
(Para 5.5 of the Report) 
 
1.21.3 The Committee takes cognizance of CAG‘s view on unutilized funds by AIIMS, Patna 
holds the Ministry equally responsible for the utter idling of funds and increase in capital 
costs. The Committee, in this connection, recommends that the Ministry should immediately 
develop a mechanism for monitoring the budget estimates and ensure utilization of funds by 
AIIMS Patna and other new AIIMS so that obstacles and hindrances, if any, are ide ntified at 
an early stage and the cost and time overrun are avoided, while implementing the project 
under PMSSY. 
 (Para 5.7 of the Report) 
Action Taken 
 
1.21.4 The main reason for under -utilisation of funds was delay in construction work related 
with hospi tal. The delays in construction also adversely impacted the equipment delivery 
schedule leading to delays in supply and installations of equipment.  Procurement of medical 
equipment‘s was also got delayed due to non -availability of faculty for most of the 
departments, leading to under -utilization of fund under GIA -capital. Substantial progress 
towards utilization of funds was made in the year 2017 -18. The unutilized balance has been 
brought down to Rs.31.37 Cr (Capital) as on March, 2018. Attempts have been made by 
Ministry to further fine -tune the releases using PFMS so as to maintain a fund balance of 
about 03 months‘ requirement.  The utilisation of funds and tracking of actual expenditure is 
being regularly monitored by the Ministry and depending on the ut ilisation, the funds are 
released. The Institute and the Ministry are taking necessary action for optimum utilization of 
funds and for effective financial monitoring mechanism.  All packages including package –I, 
II, III & IV are already completed. The pres ent progress under various packages of civil 
construction work is as below:-

1.22 STATUS OF CONSTRUCTION WORK 
 
Recommendations/Observations  
 
1.22.1 The Committee deprecates the slow pace of construction activities at AIIMS Patna. As 
per the information given by AIIMS Patna, the constructio n of hospital complex under 
Package II was originally deemed to be completed by 10th September, 2012 but even after 6 
years, the project is incomplete. Under package III and IV, the work of electrical services and 
Estate services were to be completed by De cember, 2012 and February, 2013 respectively but 
it is yet to be completed. The Committee wills to attribute the delay in execution of 
construction projects to mismanagement on the part of AIIMS, Patna. 
 (Para 5.9 of the Report) 
Action Taken 
 
1.22.2 It is submitted that the main reasons for the delay in construction was due to 
preparation of erroneous Bills of Quantities (BOQ), delays in issue of drawings by design 
consultants and delays in clearance of deviations, extra items and substituted items by Proje ct 
Consultant. Slow progress of work by contractors and abandonment of works by project 
consultants engaged initially were the other reasons for delay.  Later on, the MoH&FW 
engaged M/s HLL as the Project Consultant which helped in picking up the pace of 
construction.  At present, it is nearing completion by end of January 2022. For monitoring the 
progress of the project, MoH&FW appointed Project Consultants M/s HITES for assisting in 
timely completion of the project.  All packages including package –I, II, III & IV are already 
completed.

1.24 PROCUREMENT OF MEDICAL EQUIPMENT 
Recommendations/Observations  
 
1.24.1 The Committee fails to understand that why even simple items such as museum jar 
and LED TV were procured through sin gle bid. The Committee notes that multiple bids were 
received in these two cases but were rejected on the basis of technical specifications. The 
Committee strongly feels that technical requirements should not be misused as a means to 
limit the number of bi ds or minimize competition as is evident by the data submitted by 
AIIMS Patna. The Committee recommends that the institute should clearly specify the 
minimum technical and financial requirements ensuring an equal and fair chance to all the 
bidders. 
 (Para 5.33 of the Report) 
Action Taken 
 
1.24.2 The observation/recommendation of the Hon'ble Committee has been noted for future 
guidance. It is submitted that the new procurement policy announced by Ministry in March 

38 
 
2017, clearly states that multiple vendor pa rticipation should be encouraged. Similar 
condition has also been incorporated in Agreement with Procurement Support Agency (PSA).  
Most of the Purchase is through M/s HLL -HITES (PSA)/GEM. The purchase made outside 
these mode, the tenders are published in C PPP/Tender wizard portal/Institute website to 
ensure enough/wider participation. However, tender which end up in single bid subject to 
evaluation by the Stores Purchase Committee (SPC) and disposed of accordingly as per the 
recommendation. The Stores Purch ase Committee is also in place to ensure the procurement 
is done in line with extant policy of Government of India (GFR 2017, Manual of procurement 
of Goods, etc). Usually single bids purchases are discouraged. 
Recommendations/Observations  
 
1.24.3 The Com mittee also observes that the price at which the equipment were procured 
may not have been the best price as the Institute failed to hold a competitive procurement 
process. The Committee also desires to be apprised of the number of bidders, the reasons for  
the rejection of their proposal and the compliance proposal of the winning bidder in each case 
for procurement of museum Jar, LED TV, Co2 Laser, Carl Zesis OPMI Vario and 
Computerised Virtual Dissection Table. The Committee desires AIIMS Patna to submit a  
written technical evaluation summary for each case of proprietary/ single bid procurement till 
date to it within three months from the presentation of this Report. The Committee also 
recommends that the Director of the Institute should be made accountable for all cases of 
single bid/proprietary procurement wherever irregularities are reported /observed in future. 
 (Para 5.34 of the Report) 
 
Action Taken 
1.24.4 The observation/recommendation of the Hon'ble Committee has been noted for future  
guidance. As des ired by the Hon'ble Committee, the details of bidders, the reasons for 
rejection of their proposal, etc. in each case of procurement of the items are given separately 
in the Annexure-V(d). 
 
Recommendations/Observations  
 
1.24.5 The Committee is shocked at the audacity of the Institute by its claims to have 
purchased beds with cushions, equipment for PMR Department, two battery operated hand 
drill accessories etc with US FDA certification. As per the information submitted by AIIMS 
Patna, it procured 250 fowl er beds with cushion at a price of Rs.1.1 crore with an extra 
Comprehensive Maintenance Contract (CMC) price of Rs 24 lakhs, two battery operated hand 
drill accessories at Rs. 46 lakhs with CMC price of 7.9 lakhs, equipment for PMR Department 
at Rs. 14 lakhs with CMC price of 4.6 lakh. This superfluous spending by AIIMS Patna raises 
serious concern over the lack of monitoring of the procurement Policy. The Committee 
recommends for an enquiry into the procurement of fowler beds with cushions as these are 
easily available with Indian manufacturers at a much lower price paid by AIIMS, Patna. The 
Committee senses large irregularities in the procurement process of the Institute and seeks a 
detailed probe of all the equipment that have been procured in the last tw o years. The 

39 
 
Committee desires AIIMS Patna to submit a detailed report on the same, within three months 
of the presentation of this report to the Parliament. 
 (Para 5.37 of the Report) 
 
Action Taken 
 
1.24.6 The observation/recommendation of the Hon'ble Committee has been noted for future 
guidance. A detailed report in this regard was sent separately earlier. The  details of 
Equipment procured in the last two years are shown separately in the Annexure -V(e). 
1.25 AVAILABILITY OF BEDS AT AIIMS PATNA 
 
Recommendations/Observations  
 
1.25.1 The Committee observes with deep concern that the numbers of beds in some 
important Departments are very low. The low bed strength in each Department also reflects 
the Institute's lack of preparedness in case of an unforeseen disaster/natural calamity. Even the 
distribution of beds for various Departments appears faulty. The Committee is surprised to 
observe that Urology Department has 5 beds but the Department is not yet functional in the 
Institute. This again highlights the In stitute's failure to make optimum utilization of the 
available resources. The Committee also notes that the number of patients in Ayush 
Department is comparatively low when compared to other Departments. The Committee 
therefore is of the opinion that the b eds under Ayush Department can be used for other 
Departments on a need basis. The Committee strongly recommends that AIIMS Patna should 
carry out a detailed assessment of the patient load in different Departments and accordingly 
carry out the distribution of beds in each Department. This will certainly help in better 
management of the wards and facilitate better bed occupancy in each Department. The 
Committee recommends that the construction activities should be expedited so that the 
sanctioned bed strength of 960 is achieved in the Institute at the earliest. 
 (Para 5.65 of the Report) 
Action Taken 
 
1.25.2 The observation / recommendation of the Hon‘ble Committee has been noted for  
future guidance. Urology Department has currently 30 Functional beds. Total N o of 
sanctioned beds = 960, Total no of functional beds = 1234, The Occupancy Rate is 76.78 %.

1.27 MEDICAL IMAGING MODALITIES 
Recommendations/Observations  
 
1.27.1 The Committee is shocked at the long waiting period for Ultrasound, CT scan, and 
MRI OPD Cases. The Committee during its study visit to Patna also came across patients with 
waiting period as long as one year. The medical imaging facilities are fundamental for the 
correct diagnosis of any disease but such long waiting period render the whole medical care 
process futile and life threatening. The  Committee is not at all convinced by the plea of 
AIIMS Patna's management that shortage of MRI machines led to such long waiting period 
for MRI scans. The Committee also notes that AIIMS Patna on an average conducted only 5 
MRI per day, 2 Mammography and 18 CT scan which is very low when compared to the 
available infrastructure and manpower. The Committee believes that even with one MRI 
machine at its disposal, the Institute could have easily carried out more than twenty scans in 
one day as is being done i n other AIIMS and private hospitals. The grim data furnished by 
AIIMS Patna clearly reflects gross underutilization of the available resources and sheds light 
on the failure of the institute to work in its full capacity. The Committee during its earlier 
study visit observed that RIMS Imphal conducted 30 MRI scans per day on a very old 
machine and also carried out over 50 CT scans. The Committee desires a quarterly update 
from AIIMS Patna on the per day usage of MRI machine along with the daily pending cases . 
The Committee strongly recommends that the procurement of second MRI machine should 
only be done when the present MRI machine is optimally utilized. 
(Para 5.70 of the report) 
 
Action Taken 
 
1.27.2 As per recent records, the following is the concerned data: 
CT Scan : We have 2 CT machines out of which 1 machine is in trauma centre which runs 
24x7 of the week and has no waiting period. An average of 90-100 cases is being done on this 
machine daily. The scans being done on this machine are non -contrast studies. This machine 
is running at maximum capacity. The second machine is in the indoor department. Contrast 
studies are done on this machine which takes longer time. Average number of cases done on 
this machine is 45 -50. There is no waiting period for admitt ed or emergency patients. For 

41 
 
OPD patients, Average waiting period is 2 months. This machine is running at optimal 
capacity. 
Mammography: There is no waiting period for mammography. Cases are done on average 
between 4 to 10 cases. Patients are served as they come with no waiting period. 
MRI: Average number of cases being done is 10 -11. By virtue of AIIMS Patna being the 
highest referral centre in the region, complex studies are being performed including  cases 
which need sedation/anaesthesia and these cases require longer turnover time.

1.29 GOVERNING BODY 
Recommendations/Observations  
 
1.29.1 The Committee strongly recommends that the meetings of the Governing body, 
Academic Committee and Standing Finance Committee should be held frequently to 
discharge its entrusted responsibilities. The Committee also notes that the course of action 
decided for some agenda in the first meeting of the Governing Body had not been 
implemented even after one year and was again taken up in the second meeting of the 
Governing Body. The Committee, therefore,  feels that the Ministry must devise a follow up 
plan for the agenda discussed in such meetings. The Ministry must ensure that the decisions 
of the Governing body/Academic Committee/Standing Finance Committees are speedily 
implemented at the ground level and undue delays are avoided. The Committee also urges the 
State Government of Bihar to play a more active role in making AIIMS Patna a centre of 
excellence in medical learning and healthcare treatment. 
 (Para 5.76 of the Report) 
 
Action Taken 
 
1.29.2 On th e basis of observations of the Committee the meetings of Governing Body, 
Academic Committee are held at regular intervals.  

42 
 
1.30 MONITORING MECHANISM 
Recommendations/Observations  
 
1.30.1 The Committee is of the considered view that the success of any proj ect lies in 
effective implementation and evaluation. State Project Monitoring Committee (State PMC) 
were entrusted with the responsibility to monitor the progress of new AIIMS in the State. This 
Committee was required to meet at least once in a quarter to review the progress of AIIMS. In 
the State of Bihar no such State project Monitoring Committee (State PMC) has been 
constituted and in the absence of the required mechanism, no meetings could be conducted. 
The Committee feels that the various issues plagui ng AIIMS Patna could have been easily 
solved had the concept of State PMC was implemented on ground. 
 (Para 5.77 of the Report) 
Action Taken 
 
1.30.2 It is mentioned here that the State level project monitoring mechanism has been got 
activated through inten se engagements with the State Governments for the new AIIMS  and 
upgradation works under Phase-III and later Phases works. Presently with respect to Phase III 
and later Phases works, Project Monitoring Group (PMG) meetings under the chairmanship of 
Chief Se cretary of respective State are being held to review progress and resolve State 
specific local issues. 
E. AIIMS RAIPUR 
1.31 BUDGETARY ALLOCATION 
Recommendations/Observations  
 
1.31.1 The Committee observes with concern that the objectives of the PMSSY sche me are 
not being realized in AIIMS, Raipur because of massive underutilization of funds in the initial 
years. The utilization of the fund has constantly remained below 50 percent in all the years 
except for the year 2017 -18 where it was 56.19 percent. The Committee, further, observes 
despite the fact that there was no dearth of funds in the initial years of construction activities 
because of lack of absorption capacity of implementing agencies, technical problems and non-
availability of project consultants,  the construction work got delayed. The released funds 
under the head GIA (CCA) for two years i.e. 2015 -16 and 2016-17 were more than the funds 
sanctioned to the Institute. The main problem does not seem to be inadequate budget outlay 
but poor utilization of funds by the Institute. The Committee observes that the main reason for 
unspent funds was the slow progress of construction work. It is a matter of great concern and, 
therefore, requires introspection to assess the reasons for inability of the Institute  in judicious 
utilization of funds. 
 (Para 6.6 of the Report) 
 
 
 

43 
 
Action Taken 
 
1.31.2 Strict Monitoring has been implemented. The budget utilization has been significantly 
improved till 2023 -24. The updated position of expenditure (Utilization Certificate of F.Y. 
2023-24) is enclosed as Annexure – 6.6. 
Recommendations/Observations  
 
1.31.3 The Committee believes that the under -utilization of plan outlays by the Institute can 
be attributed to the institutional and procedural bottlenecks in the process of imp lementation 
of planned activities. The Committee also take into cognizance the observation of the CAG 
Audit Report (2018) that pointed out the Ministry had not formulated any operational 
guidelines for PMSSY, thereby, lacking in overall framework for schem e implementation. 
Approval and adoption of ad-hoc approach in term of project planning and fund management, 
delay in selection of consultant led to delay in establishment of the AIIMS. The institute did 
not obtain clear title over the land provided by the State Government for construction of 
residential complex and some Departments and centre of Institutes, that left the institute open 
to further land disputes and encroachment. Needless to say, deficiencies in the planning 
process and poor financial managem ent in the Institute caused all these sorry state of affairs. 
The Committee is of the view that effective financial management along with prudent 
budgetary projection would have enabled optimum utilization of funds. The Committee, 
therefore, recommends the  Institute to strengthen its financial management for optimum 
utilization of financial resources at the Institute. The Committee, further, recommends to the 
Institute to have effective monitoring and supervision over the implementing agencies so as to 
avoid underutilization and idling of funds. 
 (Para 6.7 of the Report) 
Action Taken 
 
1.31.4 Observation / Recommendations of the Committee have been noted for  compliance. 
Document and clear title pertaining to land of main Hospital Complex and Residential 
Complex at Kabir Nagar are available with the Institute and the same have been provided to 
CAG Audit.  Importantly, there is no encroachment in any of the allotted land.   
The State Government has already allotted 50 acres of land at Atal Nagar, Nawa Raipur to 
AIIMS Raipur on free of cost and possession of land has been taken by AIIMS, Raipur. 
Transfer of ownership and lease agreement has been done with the State Government. The 
construction of perimeter wall has been completed to prevent any encroachment.  The State 
Government is yet to hand over the land at Sarona.  The recommendation made by the 
Committee regarding strengthening financial management for optimum utilization of financial 
resources and effective monitoring and supervision over the implementing agenc ies so as to 
avoid underutilization of funds released and idling of funds in future projects/scheme is noted.

1.33 PROCUREMENT OF EQUIPMENT 
Recommendations/Observations  
 
1.33.1 The Committee observes that majority of 159 types of medical equipment during the 
last two years have been purchased with USFDA certification. The Committee is concerned at 
the substantial number of procurement done in AIIMS, Raipur with USFDA certification. The 
Committee apprehends that some of them may be available with European/Indian 
manufactures. The Committee, in this connection, reiterates its recommendation made on 
other AIIMS that the Institute must give preference to general CE/BIS certification to increase 
competitive bidding. The Committee feels that where the equipment with BIS certification is 
available, the Indian manufacturers should be given priority over the USF DA or other 
certifications. The Institute should make efforts to contribute to the ―Make in India‖, scheme 
of Government of India to give boost to Indian manufacturers. Further, USFDA certification 

45 
 
involves higher cost of equipment which would unfortunatel y be passed on to the patients 
seeking treatment and care and thereby increasing their out of pocket expenditure. The 
Committee, therefore, strongly recommends that USFDA certification may be resorted to only 
in exceptional circumstances where other manufa cturers do not match the high parameters or 
urgency of equipment with higher standard is called for. The Committee desires that the 
Institute should submit a status report on a ll medical devices/equipment procured with 
USFDA certification /single bid/propr ietary certification alongwith  reasons for doing so, 
within three months of presentation of this report to the Parliament. 
 (Para 6.31 of the Report) 
Action Taken 
 
1.33.2 The Institute follows the Public Procurement System, GFR -2017 in the procurement 
process as reiterated by it in the past and the Institute and the Ministry support the purview of 
"Make in India" policy of the Government and only in rare cases (when the equipment is not 
Make in India) the Global Tender Enquiry is resorted to by M/s H&LL. 
Recommendations/Observations 
  
1.33.3 The Committee hopes that AIIMS Raipur is adhering to procedure of multiple 
tendering in the purchase of medical equipment and avoiding single bid purchase to ensure 
transparency and public trust in procurement system of medical equipment. 
 (Para 6.32 of the Report) 
Action Taken 
 
1.33.4 AIIMS Raipur is adhering to the process of multiple tendering in the purchase of 
medical equipment and avoiding to the extent possible single bid purchase to ensure 
transparency and public trust in procurement system of medical equipment. The Institute 
follows the guidelines laid in the GFR -2017 in this respect.  Moreover, the institute is strictly 
adhering to procurements on GEM.  The Ministry issued guidelines for allowing of BIS 
standards with other standards like USFDA/ CE certification, etc. in procurement of medical 
devices/ equipment dated 20.02.2018 which has been circulated by the PMSSY Division by 
the HLL/ HITES (Procurement Support Agency)/ HSCC and all functioning AIIMS. PSA has 
intimated that they are following the standard of BIS or European CE or US FDA for PMSSY 
procurement and BIS is not an additional requirement for Indian Manufacturer who is having 
any of the certification stated above.

1.38 PROCUREMENT OF MEDICAL EQUIPMENT 
Recommendations/Observations  
 
1.38.1 The Committee takes cognizance of the problems highlighted by the Institute in the 
procurement of the equipment. The Committee observes that sometimes even after multiple 
floating of tender, only single bid is received and there are few cases when multipl e bids are 
received but only single bid gets qualified on technical grounds. The Committee believes that 
there is a need to advertise the tender to be floated on a large scale so that more and more 
manufacturers submit their bids leading to competitive pri cing and availability of many 
options for procurement of equipment. The Committee, thus, recommends to the Institute to 
advertise their tenders in the national dailies and also integrate into the system of the Central 

50 
 
Public Procurement Portal for wider di ssemination of information regarding the floating of 
tender so as to make it accessible to large number of manufacturers. The Committee, further, 
recommends to the Institute to list out the technical requirements more clearly by making 
them generic so that there are fewer chances of bids getting disqualified on technical grounds. 
The tendency to deliberately design specifications to ensure that procurement is done on 
proprietary / single bid basis should be avoided under all circumstances. The Committee 
would like to highlight that the problem areas as identified by AIIMS, Bhubaneswar are 
welcome since it would help the procedural hurdles and help in overall streamlining of the 
whole process of procurement of medical equipment. The idea of finalizing uniform  technical 
specification for requirement especially high end equipment is certainly a step in the right 
direction. The Committee strongly supports and recommends for appropriate time bound 
action on the suggestions of AIIMS, Bhubaneswar for improving the procurement process. 
 (Para 7.21 of the Report) 
Action Taken 
1.38.2 The observation / recommendation of the Hon‘ble Committee has been noted for  
future guidance.  All applicable provisions of the GFR are complied with during the 
procurement process. There ar e only some cases of single bid tender against open tenders. 
However, the same have been processed strictly as per the provision of Rule -173 (xx) of 
GFR-2017 after ascertaining that the procurement was satisfactorily advertised, and that 
sufficient time wa s given for submission of bids, the qualification criteria were not unduly 
restrictive and prices were reasonable in comparison to market values as provided under the 
Rules. Broad based technical specifications are being formulated vis -à-vis the requirement of 
the user to ensure optimal patient care.  It is further submitted that the CIB in its 2 nd meeting 
held on 10.12.2018 under Agenda No. CIB -2/7 has already approved to permit a Technical 
Expert from AIIMS/PGIMER/JIPMER/Other INIs as external experts in place of Nominee of 
DGHS for finalising technical specifications of various equipment s having estimated value of 
more than Rs.30 lakh and above.  This initiative will help to speed up the procurement 
process. Regarding the issues of finalising uniform tech nical specifications for equipment 
especially high-end equipment, it is submitted that CIB has decided to centrally procure all 
such equipment through AIIMS Delhi.  Specifications for high end equipment have been 
issued by the Ministry of Health and Family Welfare. There is no proposal now to procure 
equipments centrally through AIIMS Delhi. All Institutes are mandated to procure them on 
their own. 
Recommendations/Observations  
 
1.38.3 The Committee observes that AIIMS, Bhubaneswar has been using Tender Wiza rd for 
e-tendering for procurement of medical equipment. The Committee, therefore, desires that the 
Institute should implement/use the Central Public Procurement Portal of Government of India 
along with Tender Wizard for procurement of medical devices and equipment. The 
Committee believes that both the portals may help in facilitating all the Central Government 
Organizations to publish their tender enquiries, corrigendum and award of contract details. 
These portals bestow a single point access to the inform ation on procurements made across 
various Central Government organizations. This enables the manufacturers of the medical 

51 
 
devices and equipment to have access to the information regarding the floating of the tender 
by the Government. The Committee is of th e view that the main idea of using both the portals 
is to disseminate information about the tenders on a wide platform. 
 (Para 7.24 of the Report) 
Action Taken 
 
1.38.4 Keeping in view the observations made by the Hon‘ble Committee, AIIMS 
Bhubaneswar has already implemented the same  and now the e -tenders are also being posted 
on the Central Public Procurement Portal (CPPP), Govt. of India in addition to being 
published on Tender Wizard, institute‘s Website and in newspapers as applicable.  We have 
completely shifted to CPPP and GeM for all the procurement of Medical Devices/Equipment. 
Recommendations/Observations  
 
1.38.5 The Committee appreciates that AIIMS Bhubaneswar has procured 130 types of 
equipment with ISO/BIS certification and encourages AIIMS Bhubane swar to carry out all 
the necessary procurement from non USFDA/Indian manufacturers. The Committee, 
however, recommends AIIMS Bhubaneswar to avoid inappropriate procurement of medical 
devices/equipment with USFDA certification for basic equipment such as B ipolar Forceps, 
Microscope etc. The Committee believes that standard of quality, price and other basic 
requirements should be reviewed closely along with the endorsing the Make in India 
campaign. 
 (Para 7.26 of the Report) 
Action Taken 
 
1.38.6 Drugs Regula tion Section of the Ministry of Health & Family Welfare had issued 
guidelines dated 20.02.2018 regarding requirement/ non -requirement of USFDA/ CE 
certification, etc. in procurement of medical devices/ equipment.  The guidelines were 
circulated by the PMSSY Division of the Ministry to the HLL/ HITES - Procurement Support 
Agency (PSA)/ HSCC and to all functioning AIIMS. The PSA has intimated that they are 
following the standard of ―BIS or European CE or US FDA‖ for PMSSY procurement. 
Recommendations/Observations  
 
1.38.7 The Committee is dismayed to note that three equipments under the Cardiothoracic 
and Vascular Surgeons (CTVS) Department remained uninstalled for a period ranging from 12 
to 24 months due to non -availability of the personnel under the Departm ent. The Committee 
takes serious note of such delay in installation of equipment which may have resulted in lapse 
of warranty/guarantee of the equipment. The Committee, therefore, chides the Institute for 
delayed installation of the equipment. The Committe e, in this regard, recommends that in 
future, equipment should be procured only when the required faculty and infrastructure for the 
functioning of the equipment is available. 
 (Para 7.28 of the Report) 
 

52 
 
Action Taken

future guidance. Further, the PSA has been advised to place order for medical equipment in

have presently installed.

recommends for a common all India examination for all the non -faculty posts through a third-

transparent and objective.

1.41 MEDICAL EDUCATION 
Recommendations/Observations  
 
1.41.1 The Committee observes that while there has been 100% admission in MBBS and 
nursing course, only 45 seats have been filled up out  of the 86 sanctioned PG seats. It is 
indeed a saddening affair to observe the vacant PG seats at an Institute of national importance 
as it indicates sheer wastage of resources. The Committee, however, recommends to the 
Institute to ascertain the detailed reasons for not filling up of the PG seats  at the Institute and 
take immediate steps to remove obstacles if any, in filling up of the PG seats with immediate 
effect. 
 (Para 7.40 of the Report) 
Action Taken 
 
1.41.2 Currently out of 351 PG (MD/MS) seats 300 seats have been filled. In addition to this 
the MD/MS counselling for January 2025 session process is going on. Additionally, out of 
174 allotted DM/MCh (super specialization) seats 125 seats have been filled. In addition to 
this the DM/MCh counselling for January 2025 session process is going on. 
Recommendations/Observations  
 
1.41.3 The Committee observes that the present strength of faculty at the Institute is not 
sufficient to offer clinical training to the students. The Committee feels that deficient c linical 
training may be one of the reasons for not filling up the PG seats at the Institute. The 
Committee is of the view that clinical training is crucial for emphasizing the overall 
development of the medical insight of the students. A medical student wi ll become more 
efficient and productive if he/she is trained well. The Committee, thus, recommends to the 
Institute to make the clinical training programme more effective with sufficient faculty and 
adequate infrastructure for training to the students. 
 (Para 7.42 of the Report) 

54 
 
Action Taken 
1.41.4 AIIMS, Bhubaneswar makes all efforts to impart quality clinical training to all PG 
students besides training them in soft skills such as communication, ethical and professional 
conduct, rational prescribing, patient safety etc. Research methodology and basic biostatistics 
is also taught to them and they are mandated to undertake research in the form of a thesis in 
partial fulfilment of their degree. Undergraduate medical students have evening clinics in 
addition t o their routine postings, clerkship and first aid classes in surgery and conduct 
deliveries (in final year and in internship) both at AIIMS Bhubaneswar and at the District 
Hospital, Kurdha, so that they are fully confident to conduct normal deliveries inde pendently. 
In order to ensure that undergraduates have tolerant the necessary competencies, an exit 
Objective Structured Clinical Examination (OSCE) is held during the last month of internship 
in which students rotate through 25 stations wherein their clin ical skills are tested. This was 
started in December 2017 for the first batch of interns and is continuing since. Recently, small 
grants as incentives for student research have been approved by CIB to be given to PG and 
UG students. All academic activity, besides clinical training is done by the faculty of the 
Institute and the infrastructure is adequate for such training. The UG curriculum has been 
modified to include communication skills, patient safety, ethics, and professionalism and 
changes made to tea ching-learning activities and assessment formats. The PG curriculum is 
also being modified at present to make it competency -based.  The institute is taking all steps 
to ensure that students of AIIMS Bhubaneswar have stimulating, constructive, well -rounded 
training during their stint here. In addition, as the Institute has advertised 125 vacant faculty 
posts to deploy more faculty members, this will further improve the clinical training. 
1.42 STATUS OF OPERATION THEATRES AND WARDS/BEDS 
Recommendations/Observations  
 
1.42.1 The Committee is not satisfied with the functional status of the Institute as only 32 
wards with 716 beds are functional at the Institute. The Institute is also not properly equipped 
to cater to the need for a quick and responsive care in m edical emergency. Good trauma care 
means getting the patient to the right place at the right time for appropriate care. The 
Committee, thus, recommends to the Institute to step up the strength of wards and beds as per 
the sanctioned strength. It also needs to expedite the process of construction of remaining OTs 
equipped with latest equipment, specialized resources, and a specially trained trauma and 
emergency team for quality and timely care. It is more so required for treatment of the 
patients suffering f rom life threatening illnesses and traumatic injuries. The Committee, 
therefore, expects that concerted efforts will be made in this regard and the matter should be 
taken up on a priority basis. 
 (Para 7.47 of the Report) 
Action Taken 
 
1.42.2 At present, 1370 are functional at AIIMS Bhubaneswar.

Centre beds including 12 ICU beds (out of sanctioned beds 86) + 17 Burn

G. UPGRADATION OF GOVERNMENT MEDICAL COLLEGES (GMCs) / 
INSTITUTIONS 
1.44 BUDGETARY ALLOCATION AND FUNDING PATTERN 
Recommendations/Observations  
 
1.44.1 The Committee is of the view that the funds allocated to the project should be 
effectively and efficiently utilized and not remain idle or underutilized as that would 
otherwise adversely impact the smooth execution of the project. Mor eover, such instances 
reflect faulty planning and improper administration with regard to utilization of the allocated 
funds. The Committee views this as a matter of concern. The Committee observes that 
upgradation of GMCs is under the direct supervision of  the Central Government , therefore, 
underutilization of the allocated funds should not become a routine affair as the same would 
lead to further delays in completion of projects causing time and cost overruns. The 
Committee, therefore, strongly recommends that the Ministry must act promptly and monitor 
the effective and timely utilization of funds. 
 (Para 8.11 of the Report) 
 

56 
 
Action Taken 
 
1.44.2 Observations / Recommendations of the Committee have been noted for compliance . 
It is submitted that the release  of funds to Executing Agencies (EA) for various projects are 
made only after obtaining utilization certificates from the respective EA. Further, regular 
review meetings are being held with project authorities, including the executing agencies for 
timely utilization of funds. 
Recommendations/Observations  
 
1.44.3 The Committee views diversion of funds as criminal misappropriation of allocated 
fund and violation of fiscal discipline.  Such trend not only results in the shortfall of the funds 
for completion o f construction activities but is against the provisions of the MoU signed 
between the Ministry and the States.   Effective monitoring and accountability need to be 
ensured so that funds released are utilized for the intended purpose.   The Committee expects 
the Ministry to issue strict advisories against the diversion of funds and fixing of 
responsibility of GMCs/ State Government for the same. 
  (Para 8.12 of the Report) 
Action Taken 
 
1.44.4 Observations / Recommendations of the Committee have been noted for  compliance. 
It is submitted that the permissible diversion will be accounted for by modifying the DPR 
accordingly and States will be asked to refund the amount spent on impermissible diversions 
at the time of closure report of an upgradation project. It i s submitted that, the Ministry has 
taken up with the respective State Governments /   Institutes for completion and settlement of 
the matter.  State Governments have also been advised to send proposal for seeking 
modification of DPR with proper justification. Presently, with a view to prevent recurrence of 
such cases as above, as a Policy construction / procurement matters are now being dealt 
through EA / PSA appointed by the Govt. of India, Ministry of Health & Family Welfare 
only. The Ministry has already  taken needed systemic measures to ensure that the issues 
highlighted by the Committee are adequately addressed.  Further learning from the past 
experience of Phase I and II works, the Ministry now gets all procurement (except small value 
items below Rs.30 lakh) made through a PSA appointed by the Ministry. The provision to this 
effect has been incorporated in the MOU signed with the State Governments. This has given 
better control in the hands of the Ministry to ensure that the money is spent only for the 
purpose for which it is sanctioned and any possibility of diversion of fund is obviated. 
 
1.45 KEY CONCERN AREAS 
Recommendations/Observations  
 
1.45.1 The Committee takes note of the key concern areas of GMCs upgradation. The 
Committee observes that the Mi nistry has put the entire onus on the State Governments for 
successful implementation of the scheme. The Committee is of the view that the Central 
Government has a key role in planning, guiding and monitoring the scheme. The Ministry 
needs to consider Stat e Governments as its partner in resolving the constraints being 

57 
 
encountered in administration of projects and sort out practical difficulties faced in 
implementation thereof. There is a need for an efficacious monitoring mechanism to avoid 
inordinate delays in completion of upgradation projects. The Committee, thus, recommends to 
the Ministry to make a realistic assessment of the challenges faced and formulate suitable plan 
of action in active coordination with the State Governments to overcome the same. Al l out 
efforts must be made by both the Central Government and the State Governments to strive 
towards completion of upgradation of GMCs within specified time line. 
  (Para 8.16 of the Report) 
Action Taken 
 
1.45.2 Observations / Recommendations of the Commi ttee have been noted for compliance.  
The key concern areas and challenges faced in up -gradation of the GMC Projects are (i) 
Timely availability of Encumbrance free site in the GMCs, time taken in deciding on the 
scope of the Up -gradation projects in terms of the physical facilities and difficulty faced by 
the GMC/ State Government to firm up the scope of the Project resultantly the process of gap 
analysis, DPR Preparation and tendering had to be carried all over again in such cases. 
Commitment by the State Government is also a key concern and also the delay in releasing 
the State Government‘s share (40%) despite the commitment in the MOU signed by Ministry 
of Health with the State Government. The issue has been taken up at senior level of the State 
Governments through letters and over phone and the States have also been taking action for 
early release of the Share. However, Ministry has released timely of the Central Share to the 
Executing Agencies and to the Procurement Support Agency. The Ministry has also taken up 
with the State Governments in meetings, Video -Conferencing and through DO Letters and 
also in the PMC Meetings. The issues and hindrances were also flagged in the Ministerial 
level interactions with the States at the level; of Hon‘ble Health & Fam ily Welfare Minister 
and Hon‘ble MOS at present all issues pertaining to all the 39 Phase III up -gradation projects 
have been sorted out and the construction is progressing well.  As of now, all the State 
Governments have released their share of funds eithe r partially or fully.  Out of 39 GMC 
projects under Phase III 32 have been completed and another 06 expected to be completed by 
2021-22 and 01 in 2022-23. In addition to the information earlier provided, it is stated that all 
39 projects covered under Phase-III of the Scheme have now been completed. 
 
Recommendations/Observations  
 
1.45.3 The attention of the Committee has been drawn to the CAG findings wherein serious 
areas of concern have been highlighted especially w.r.t non -compliance of codal and contrac t 
provision and awards of work at higher cost. The Committee is of the firm view that strict 
compliance with codal and contract provision need to be adhered in all circumstances and 
award of work should be assigned on merit and proven track record. 
  (Para 8.17 of the Report) 
Action Taken 
 
1.45.4 It has already been submitted to CAG that the appointment of the Consultants on 
nomination basis for the different works has been made in the light of the provisions under

Consultants and under the CPWD Manual. Due approval of the Hon‘ble Minister was also 
obtained for this purpose. Accordingly, the action on the part of the Ministry was proper as 
per the extant rules. Accordi ngly, HLL/HITES/HSCC as 100% owned PSUs of the Ministry, 
have been assigned roles of Project Consultant/Procurement Support Agent/in House 
Consultant/Executing Agency in the PMSSY Project after careful consideration in the given 
circumstances with a view to achieve the objective. Engaging a private or another party would 
not have provided same efficiency and flexibility as could have been obtained by having these 
PSUs. Both the PSUs have required experience in Hospital Projects. It has already been 
submitted to CAG that the appointment of the Consultants on nomination basis for the 
different works has been made in the light of the provisions under GFR 176 and as per Rule 
1.5.3 of the Manual of Policies and Procedure for selection of the Consultants and under  the

roles of Project Consultant/Procurement Support Agent/in House Consultant/Executing

same efficiency and flexibility as could have been obtained by having these PSUs. Both the 
PSUs have required experience in Hospital Projects. 
 
1.46 SELECTION OF THE GMCs FOR UPGRADATION 
Recommendations/Observations  
 
1.46.1 The Committee fe els that the Ministry has not devised scientific criteria/parameters 
for the selection of GMCs under PMSSY. The Committee feels that the criteria of socio -
economic indicators, mainly the presence of already existing tertiary healthcare facilities, 
should also be taken into account. The Committee also take into cognizance that many GMCs 
have been selected without consulting the respective States and even without considering their 
financial condition and even without entering into any MoU with the State Gover nments. 
Further, the selection of the consultancy agencies was purely arbitrary and no past record of 
the agencies has been taken into consideration. To consider HLL/HSCC as a panacea for all 
execution is an area of serious concern as these agencies are be ing given preferential 
treatment just because they are PSUs of the Ministry not because of their proven track record. 
The Committee, thus, recommends to the Ministry to formulate set parameters for selection of 
GMCs to ensure that the GMCs which actually r equire upgradation are selected in 
consultation with State Governments. The Committee, further, recommends that the Ministry, 
while appointing consultancy agencies, should also take into consideration past reputation and 
good track record of the agencies i n order to avoid time and cost overruns. Accordingly, the 
Committee recommends for involvement of better professional agencies or other reputed 
construction majors to ensure cost effective and timely execution especially in projects where 
HSCC has failed t o ensure timely completion of the construction projects. The Committee 
also takes a serious note on part of the PSA in undertaking a proper gap analysis in GMCs 
where requisite medical equipment were not provided on a timely basis. 
  (Para 8.23 of the Report) 
 

59 
 
Action Taken 
1.46.2 It is submitted that, PMSSY aims at correcting the regional imbalances in the 
availability of the tertiary healthcare and medical education in different parts of the country. 
In fact, there is deficiency in availability of overall quality tertiary healthcare and medical 
education facilities in the country. The Ministry has its own database under National Health 
Mission on penetration and reach of healthcare throughout the country. Each State in the 
country also vies for central assi stance in setting up of more medical facilities in their 
respective States.  The Ministry has, while finalizing the upgradation projects given higher 
priority to the States generally considered ―backward‖ with respect to the broad socio -
economic indicators. The State has to agree to operate and maintain the facilities created, so 
willingness of the State to contribute it‘s share and participate in the scheme is also important 
apart from backwardness. As per the EFC notes available with the Ministry, all proj ects have 
been selected on the basis of demands raised by States and therefore the States were fully 
involved in the selection process. 
 
1.46.3 It is further submitted that while allocation of the works in Phase III of up -gradation, 
the past performance of  the agencies were considered. The fact is that the Ministry has been 
closely interacting and monitoring the progress of the works and performance of Ministry‘s 
own PSUs. Every year an MOU is signed with these CPSEs and their performance is carefully 
appraised against MOU parameters in a structured review, ultimately resulting in MOU 
ratings which are well documented. For every decision there is no need to depict the 
performance detail separately as, being Ministry‘s own PSUs, their performances had always 
been under scrutiny in the Ministry.  Even in case of Phase -III works assigned to the above 
two PSUs, their performance as Executing agency has been satisfactory. In almost all cases, 
these PSUs have been prompt in awarding the contracts and good progress h as been achieved 
by them. PSA is responsible for survey, gap analysis and preparation of list of requirements in 
consultation with the concerned Institution. Therefore, the Institutions are also involved in the 
process of gap analysis.  It is also brought o ut here that the delays in Phase -III works have 
primarily occurred due to delays on the part of the State Government/ GMCs in making 
available the encumbrance -free sites. In some cases, delay has occurred due to the State 
Government insisting on changes in  the already approved scope of the project or shifting the 
already approved sites.

the agency with the most economical and best offer of  services is awarded the tender for 
completion of the projects.

delays in the completion of the projects. Procedural and preparatory exercises need to be done 
before hand including availability of encumbrance free site by the respective State 
Government. The Com mittee also recommends to the Ministry to frame operational 
guidelines that would guide and regulate implementation of the scheme across the States and 
take steps to expedite the completion of remaining work by better monitoring of the projects. 
The Commit tee apprehends that such delays will increase the work load at the flag end of 
completion schedule. The Committee, therefore, is of the considered view that priority should 
be given to such projects for which there is imminent need to expedite the process of granting 
necessary approvals only to avoid time and cost overruns are avoided. The Committee also 
strongly recommends that no new project be taken before completion of these 73 projects. 
  
 (Para 8.32 of the Report) 
Action Taken 
 
1.47.4 The Recommendations of the Committee have been noted for guidance. 
 
Recommendations/Observations  
 
1.47.5 The Committee understands that there may be certain practical difficulties that are 
encountered on work sites during the course of upgradation project. However, the 
considerable delays in planning and commencement of works in all phases of upgradation of 
GMCs under PMSSY and the slow progress, both at the stage of planning and contracting 
indicates that both planning of works and contract management were inadequate and  
improper, thus causing adverse impact in the delivery of services. The Committee is of the 
firm view that the Ministry/Central Government, instead of taking the responsibility, has 
shifted the blame on the State Governments for the delay in the commenceme nt of 
upgradation of GMCs under PMSSY. The Committee feels that instead of blame game, the 
Ministry should work in tandem with the State Governments and help in ironing out problem 
areas so that the objectives of the scheme is not defeated by delays and fa ulty planning. The 
Committee, therefore, recommends to the Ministry to review the status of construction 
timelines and set realistically achievable targets in consultation with the State Governments 
concerned. 
  (Para 8.33 of the Report)

Projects. Also, the State Level Project Monitoring Committees also monitor the progress of

fortnightly review meetings are also being held with the Executing Agencies (EAs) to review 
the progress of works.  Construction timeline have been reviewed and realistic timelines 
drawn.  
 
1.48 PROCUREMENT OF EQUIPMENT 
Recommendations/Observations  
 
1.48.1 The Committee notes with concern the observations of CAG regarding improper gap 
analysis, lacunae in CMC provisions and procurement of equipment which did not fall in the 
approved list of medical equipment of the Minist ry. The CAG Report further highlights that 
against the procurement orders for 818 equipment costing Rs. 482.21 crore for 11 GMCs, 191 
equipment costing Rs. 51.72 crore were yet to be made available. The Committee is 
concerned to note that there are several  tenders which are pending since very long and are 
being retendered again and again. The Committee notes that the basic purpose of having a 
PSA stands defeated if such delays are going to occur. The Committee, in this connection, 
strongly recommends for a properly monitored mechanism, so as to ensure that time and cost 
overruns in the procurement process are avoided. The Committee is surprised to note that a 
total of 141 tenders for the procurement of equipment are pending under phase III of the 
upgradation project under PMSSY. The Committee observes that these 141 tenders have been 
retendered by the Ministry in the year 2018. Out of these, 4 tenders for surgical loupes, extra 
corporeal membrane oxygenator, head saver and surgical xenon head light and light sources 
have again been floated. Also, 24 types of equipment under phase I and II and 7 types of 
equipment under phase III have been procured on a single bid basis. The Committee desires 
the PSA to submit a status note in all the procurement carried out on single bid basis in Phase-
I and Phase -II including reasons for the same, within three months of presentation of this 
report. 
 
  (Para 8.41 of the Report) 
Action Taken 
1.48.2 It is submitted that the progress of procurement of Medical Equipment by PSA has 
been monitored closely.  Considerable progress has been achieved in the procurement of 
medical equipment for Phase I, II and III up -gradation project. Almost all the medical 
equipment under Phase I and II have since been procured and installed also.  For Ph ase III 
also the procurement activities are in a very advanced stage as brought out below: 
 

62 
 
As per information supplied by the Procurement Support Agency, all the equipment which 
was ordered for 11 GMCs referred to in the said para of CAG report has alread y been 
delivered and installed. Further, Integrated Purchase Committee meetings are regularly held 
along with review with Institutes. The delivery and installations in many instances depends on 
site clearance for installation, availability of faculty to ch eck the installed equipment and 
other factors. 
 
1.49 SUPER SPECIALTY BLOCKS 
 
Recommendations/Observations  
 
1.49.1 The Committee is distressed to observe that out of the GMCs (58) approved under first 
three phases of the upgradation project, nine GMCs do n ot have Super Specialty Services. 
These are Institute of Medical Sciences, BHU, Varanasi (Ph.I), Jawaharlal Nehru Medical 
College, AMU, Aligarh (Ph.II), Srinagar Medical College (Ph.I), B.J. Medical College, 
Ahmadabad (Ph.I),Grant Medical College, Mumbai ( Ph.I), GMC, Nagpur (Ph.II), Govt. 
Medical College, Malda (PH III), Vijayanagar Institute of Medical Sciences, Bellary (PH III), 
Pt. B.D. Sharma PGIMS, Rohtak (Ph.II). The Committee, further, notes that none of the 
GMCs approved under phase I, II and III ha s all the Super Specialty facilities as originally 
envisaged under the upgradation project. The Committee apprehends that unless the 
upgradation projects under the phase III is not completed, the Ministry would not be in a 
position to focus on GMCs in the later phases. The Committee is of the considered view that 
the scheme would be successful only when Super Specialty Blocks (SSB) envisaged under the 
scheme are functional in all the GMCs approved for upgradation. The Committee, therefore, 
recommends the Ministry to focus on the non-functional SSBs of GMCs for capacity building 
to provide super -specialty services to suffering patients. The Committee also strongly 
recommends for a gap analysis in the availability of super -speciality services in GMCs in 
locations where there are functional regional AIIMS or new ones are proposed. 
  (Para 8.51 of the Report) 
 
Action Taken 
 
1.49.2 The Committees recommendations have been noted for compliance.  The upgradation 
programme under PMSSY broadly envisages improving infra structure through Super 
Speciality Block/Trauma Care Centre etc. and procurement of medical equipment for existing 
as well as new facilities. Trauma Centres have been created in Eight Medical Colleges, except 
PGIMS, Rohtak where OT Complex covering 16 OTs is being constructed.  Further under 
Phase V, SSB is being constructed at IMS, BHU, Varanasi. 
 
1.50 MONITORING MECHANISM 
Recommendations/Observations  
 
1.50.1 The Committee is dissatisfied with the response of the Ministry regarding the existing 
institutional arrangement for monitoring the implementation of upgradation Scheme of 
Government Medical Colleges under PMSSY. The existing monitoring mechanism to review 
the progress through an online dashboard and Project Management Software by the executive 

63 
 
agencies is not effective enough to ensure timely execution of upgradation projects. In this 
regard, the Committee also notes with concern the observation of CAG made in its report that 
in eight GMCs mandatory State PMCs under the Chairmanship of Principal Secret ary 
(Health/Medical Education), to review the progress of upgradation scheme have not been 
constituted. Similarly, in many States, a State Level Steering Committee, under the 
Chairmanship of Chief Secretary, required to meet each quarter to review progress  and 
resolve local issues were either not constituted or if constituted were not active. Utmost to the 
surprise of the Committee, the Ministry has no record about the working of these Committees. 
Moreover, the Ministry's direction to have a system of Third Party Quality Assurance (TPQA) 
has also not been complied with in 15 GMCs. The Committee during its visit to West Bengal 
and Bihar found the absence of these Committees in various GMCs, therefore, the Committee 
concludes that ‗all is not well‘ with the mo nitoring system over the execution of programmes 
for upgradation schemes of GMCs under the PMSSY. The Committee, therefore, strongly 
recommends that mandatory provision of constitution of PMCs, State Level Steering 
Committees must be complied with and the system of Third Party Quality Assurance (TPQA) 
be placed in order to make resilient monitoring mechanism for efficient and effective 
execution of upgradation projects in all GMCs. 
  (Para 8.58 of the Report) 
 
Action Taken 
 
1.50.2 It is submitted that the S tatus/Progress of construction time lines and deadlines are 
being reviewed by the Ministry at Project Monitoring Committee Meetings held from time to 
time with the concerned stakeholders by the Ministry. The Monitoring and Evaluation of the 
progress of the GMCIs are done by the Project Monitoring Committee. The PMC held many 
Meetings for effective monitoring, supervision and coordination of GMC Projects. Also, the 
State Level Project Monitoring Committees monitors the progress of the GMC Projects. The 
Ministry has actively engaged with the State Governments through meetings, discussion and 
written communication, Video -conferencing with States. PMC Meetings were also held to 
resolve issues and hindrances on part of State Government. With respect to Phase III and later 
Phase works, Project Monitoring Group (PMG) meeting under the Chairmanship of Chief 
Secretary of respective States are also being held to review progress and resolve local issues. 
The progress of the Projects is also being monitored through a Das hboard created in the 
Ministry for all PMSSY works. Further, fortnightly review meetings at the Ministry level are 
also being held at the Executing Agencies (EAs) to review the progress of works.  It is a fact 
that the Independent Third Party Quality Assura nce Audit in GMCIs system was not put in 
place in the Phase I and II works. However, from Phase -III works onwards, independent 
TPQAs have been appointed under PMSSY to have independent assessment and evaluation of 
quality of construction works being executed by the executive agencies. 
 
 
 
 
 
 

64 
 
1.51 STUDY VISIT OF THE COMMITTEE 
 
Recommendations/Observations  
 
1.51.1 The Committee, therefore, is of the view that actual condition of the upgradation 
project in GMCs is totally different from what is reflected on pa per. The Committee feels that 
even after the utilization of the allocated funds there is no perceptible change at the ground 
level which clearly means the Government funds have not been properly utilized under the 
project. The Committee, therefore, recomme nds to the Ministry to ensure adherence to 
contract provisions in execution of work and provision of services. The Committee feels that 
accountability needs to be fixed for expenditure without adequate justification or where 
Government funds have not been utilized properly or when there is misappropriation of funds. 
Moreover, the Committee would like to reiterate that professional agencies with a proven 
track record must be involved in the construction process so as to avoid building flaws 
leading to wasteful expenditure. 
  (Para 8.60 of the Report) 
 
Action Taken 
 
1.51.2 These observations of the Committee pertain to the super -speciality block which was 
getting constructed under the ―Forward linkage scheme‖ under NHM. The building had 
remained incomplete due  to exhaustion of the sanctioned funds. The construction of this 
super-speciality block was being done by a State Government agency. The concerns 
/observations of the Committee have been forwarded to the State Government for future 
guidance and for taking remedial action. So far as the construction of the Super -Speciality 
Block (SSB) under the PMSSY scheme is concerned, the funds have been properly utilized. 
The super-speciality block is completed.  
 
1.52 CONCLUSION 
 
Recommendations/Observations  
 
1.52.1 The Committee observes that upgradation project of GMCs under PMSSY has failed 
to provide the desired results which were expected from the project. The Committee opines 
that there is a big gap between planning and the execution of the project due to lack of 
synchronization and coordination of activities and serious gaps between planning and 
implementation of the scheme. The Committee feels that the inadequate project management 
and related delays impacted the delivery of envisaged benefits in terms of improve ment in 
health infrastructure. The reasons for delay in upgradation project are mainly due to non -
availability of encumbrance free land, delays in getting clearances for excavation and tree 
cutting and other site related conditions, changes made in the sco pe and quantum of work, 
delay in finalization/tendering of medical equipment. Apart from all these, poor planning and 
coordination contributed to delays in upgradation of GMCs. The Committee appreciates the 
potential in the scheme to transform the Governme nt Medical Colleges in providing quality 
health services to the people. The Committee is therefore of the firm view that projects 

65 
 
require meticulous planning and consistent efforts for timely execution/completion. The 
Committee believes that preparatory ac tivities should be completed beforehand which should 
include realistic assessment of requirement of equipment, availability of site, analysis of 
manpower and utilization of funds for the purpose it is earmarked. The Committee 
recommends to the Ministry to work in tandem with the State Governments to work in a  
coordinated manner to accomplish the objective and mission of up - gradation of GMCs as 
envisaged in PMSSY. 
  (Para 8.67 of the Report) 
 
Action Taken 
 
1.52.2 As per Memorandum of Understanding signed be tween the Ministry of Health and 
Family Welfare and the concerned State Government for upgradation of GMCIs under the 
PMSSY, the responsibility of providing encumbrances -free land rests with the concerned 
State Government. Given the fact that the State Gov ernment is advised to provide the land for 
development of campus, invariably some hindrances, both natural and manmade like nalah, 
road, pipelines, etc are encountered. As the delay in land availability and securing all statutory 
clearances for the project s are critical conditions precedent for the start of any project, the 
Ministry actively coordinates with the concerned State Government authorities for  timely 
resolution of such land related issues and statutory clearances.  
 
H. OBSERVATIONS/RECOMMENDATIONS ON COMMON ISSUES 
 
1.53 FINANCIAL ASSESSMENT 
Recommendations/Observations  
 
1.53.1 The Committee is taken aback at this skewed financial assessment of the 
Government‘s flagship health care service programme. This fluctuating assessment speaks 
volume of the Ministry‘s lack in planning and failure to formulate any operational guidelines 
for AIIMS like Institutions. The Committee also notes that the approval of the revised cost 
was obtained in March, 2010 i.e. after four years of the initial approval in 2006.  This 
represents a 188% and 145% increase in capital costs since 2004 and 2006 respectively. The 
Committee, therefore, recommends that the Ministry must undertake proper and practical 
financial assessment with respect to the capital cost of new AIIMS like institutions in the 
subsequent phases. 
  (Para 9.3 of the Report) 
Action Taken 
 
1.53.2 Observations / Recommendations of the Committee have been noted for future 
compliance. However, as per the Revised Cost Estimates (RCE) approved by CCEA in 2010 
the reasons for cost escalation provided for are tabulated below: 
Sl. Items considered for cost

(i)

approximately Rs.70 crore.
