will increase by approximately Rs 9 crore. 
 
As may be seen from the above, the change in scope and inclusion of additional items, in 
facilities and medical equipment were the major contributors to the cost escalation. To 
prevent occurrence of such cost escalation, the Ministry has standardized the facilities as well 
as Medical equipment in each AIIMS, the DPR, are also prepared with greater rigor after 
carrying out of soil surveys and topographical survey. 
1.54 SITE SELECTION OF AIIMS LIKE INSTITUTIONS 
Recommendations/Observations  
 
1.54.1 The Committee is of the view that site selection for any AIIMS like Institution 
requires a detailed assessment of the geographical location and the demographic distribution. 
Easy accessibility and transportation availability is one major concern. The Committee is also 
concerned at the duplication and concentration of tertiary care services at a particular place. 
The Committee notes that at several places like Guwahati, Patna and now in Madurai (where 
a new AIIM S has just been announced) the Government had already selected GMCs for 
upgradation programme under PMSSY. In the places that have been selected for setting up of 
AIIMS like Institutions, the critical gap in the tertiary health care is already being filled  by 
private sector. The Committee strongly believes that AIIMS should be located in an area with 
low Human Development Index that lags behind in tertiary health care. 
  (Para 9.4 of the Report) 
Action Taken 
 
1.54.2 Observations / Recommendations of the Committee have been noted for future 
guidance. The Ministry while finalizing the upgradation projects has given higher priority to 
the States generally considered ―backward‖ with respect to the broad socio -economic 
indicators. The State has also agreed to ope rate and maintain the facilities created, so 

67 
 
willingness of the State to contribute its share and participate in the scheme is also important 
factor apart from backwardness. As per the EFC notes available with the Ministry, all projects 
have been selected on the basis of demands raised by States and therefore the States were 
fully involved in the selection process. 
 
1.54.3 The Ministry has its own database under National Health Mission on penetration and 
reach of healthcare facilities throughout the country . Each State in the country also vies for 
central assistance in setting up of more medical facilities in their respective States.  The 
Ministry has also adopted the ―Challenge method criteria‖ as conveyed by Cabinet Secretary 
(vide D.O. 272/1/3/2016 – CA.V dated 20.9.2017) for selection of sites so that most suitable 
sites get selected for setting up of AIIMS.  ―Gaps in the availability of tertiary healthcare 
facilities is also a consideration under the ―Challenge method‖ in selection of Sites. However, 
the M inistry is also dependent on the States in this matter as, in the first place, the States 
offers alternative sites and a site can only be selected out of the sites offered.  The ―Challenge 
method Criteria‖ referred above is being strictly followed in the ma tter of selection of sites 
out of the sites offered by the State Governments. 
 
Recommendations/Observations  
 
1.54.4 Attention of the Committee has also been drawn to a strange criterion for selection of 
site for AIIMS like Institute. Air connectivity to t he place has been one of the major basis for 
site selection for AIIMS. It has been reasoned out that airport services will facilitate 
employment of better trained human resources as most doctors are reluctant to serve in remote 
areas. 
 
(Para 9.5 of the Report) 
 
1.54.5 The Committee observes that all the six AIIMS under Phase I are well connected and 
are major cities in their respective States but still these new AIIMS are plagued by acute 
shortage of manpower. There is no faculty from AIIMS Delhi or other p remier Institutes 
working full time as Head of Department or as Professors in the new AIIMS. Therefore, the 
Committee is of the opinion that the perception that availability of an airport facility results in 
faculty retention is based on a false premise. T he Ministry and the Institute could have 
addressed this issue with better manpower planning rather than focusing on feasibility of 
airport. 
 
  (Para 9.6 of the Report) 
 
Action Taken 
 
1.54.6 AIIMS is a tertiary care Institute hence quality of doctors and me dical personnel is of 
paramount importance. It is also a fact, that availability of quality faculty, medical personnel, 
engineering and others is the most crucial impediment in functionalization of the AIIMS. 
Considering that most of the faculties and othe rs are recruited from existing Institutes of 
National Importance (INIs) / prestigious Institutes; they are not based in site locations of 

68 
 
respective AIIMS. Hence to facilitate their movement one of the criterions for setting up of 
AIIMS considers air connectivity of that particular location.  
 
1.54.7 In new AIIMS, most of faculty who have joined are trained in INIs like AIIMS, New 
Delhi/PGIMER/JIPMER/NIMHANS and other reputed Institutes like SGPGI & CMC, 
Vellore at their PG level or as SR / Faculty or in ot her capacity. Many of the Head of the 
Department in AIIMS are from different INIs or from other reputed Institutes.  Air 
connectivity is one of the many issues plaguing the availability of faculty in these Institutes. 
From the faculty perspective, there are  other constraints like availability of quality 
infrastructure like Schools, Colleges and Higher Education etc. at par with metros. Hence, 
faculties from AIIMS, Delhi or other premier Institutes do not generally prefer to join these 
Institutes as facilit ies are not at par with Delhi. Hence, there are multiple reasons for which 
availability of faculty suffers. Attracting the best Talent for the Medical faculty positions is of 
paramount necessity and apart from the aspects like Air connectivity being one of t hem, best 
Schooling facilities and Higher Education facilities, Infrastructural facilities/support systems, 
recreational and Entertainment facilities are important for life beyond office hours. 
 
 
Recommendations/Observations  
 
1.54.8 The Committee strongly recommends that the Ministry while deciding the location of 
the new AIIMS must take note of the already existing healthcare infrastructure in the vicinity 
of the earmarked area so that duplication of healthcare facilities are avoided. The healthcare 
system should be such that delivery of medical facility and care is regionally distributed and 
even the remotest region is well within its outreach so as to address regional imbalances in 
provision of health care facilities. 
  (Para 9.7 of the Report) 
Action Taken 
 
1.54.9 Observations / Recommendations of the Committee have been noted for future  
guidance. Under the challenge method, the availability of already existing Health 
infrastructure is also taken into consideration. 
 
1.55 SELECTION OF PROJECT CONSULTANTS/CONTRACTORS 
 
Recommendations/Observations  
 
1.55.1 The Committee during the examination of the Demands for Grants has repeatedly seen 
unprofessionalism and delay in almost all the projects being executed by HSCC. During the 
course of its study visit to Gu wahati the Committee had a firsthand view of the manner in 
which improper planning by HSCC had resulted in major design flaws. The Committee, 
therefore, strongly feels that the Ministry should follow a fair and transparent method for the 
selection of the p roject consultants and ensure that the agencies involved are technically 
competent to complete such big infrastructure projects, given the mixed track record of 
HLL/HSCC in other projects. 
  (Para 9.10 of the Report) 

69 
 
Action Taken 
 
1.55.2 Observations / Recommendations of the Committee have been noted. The new AIIMS 
works are now being assigned to empanelled PSUs having experience of building large 
hospitals and other building infrastructure. The PSUs are appointed as Executing Agencies 
(EAs) with full empowerment to take decisions on all contractual matters including power to 
award construction tenders. With this arrangement, the contractual matters need not have to 
come to Ministry for decision thus simplifying the process and also avoiding delays. The 
system of appointing Project Consultants (PC) has been discontinued in view of the 
unsatisfactory experience with Project Consultants in the first six AIIMS Projects. The system 
of assigning work to Executing Agencies (EAs) has been put in place since 2015 and  all new 
AIIMS and GMC upgradation work for PMSSY Phase III onwards have been assigned to 
Executing Agencies under this system. Further, since 2016 the New AIIMS works are being 
executed by the Executive Agencies in ―Engineering, Procurement and Constructi on (EPC)‖ 
mode. In the EPC system, the construction agency itself is responsible for detailed designing 
and engineering and thus the problems of coordination between the Design Consultant and the 
Construction agency and consequent risk of delays also gets systemically addressed. 
 
Recommendations/Observations  
 
1.55.3 The Committee observes that in all the new AIIMS like institutions, undue delay in 
construction activities resulted in cost and time overrun. It is surprising to note that the 
timeline for completion of the construction project was not met in any of the new AIIMS and 
was further extended by 5 to 7 years. CAG in its Report No 10 had also observed that the 
scheduled dates of completion of six new AIIMS of Phase -I were between August 2011 and 
July 2013. Construction works were not completed by scheduled dates in any of the new 
AIIMS and there were delays of about four to five years. 
(Para 9.11 of the Report) 
 
1.55.4 The Committee also observes that there were major architectural design faults in the  
buildings which in turn led to more delays and escalated the cost. A lot of time had to be 
invested in correcting the structural flaws caused due to the inconsiderate design planning. 
Moreover, various arbitration cases led to stalling of the project as t he companies waited for 
dispute resolutions and settlements. The Committee also notes that till date, only AIIMS 
Bhubaneswar has completed the construction activities under all the four packages.  
 
(Para 9.12 of the Report) 
 
1.55.5 The Committee is aggriev ed at the failure of the Ministry to monitor the timely 
completion of the project, mainly the construction activities in all the new AIIMS like 
institutions. The Ministry should also ensure proper planning and timely execution for 
completion of project. Th e Institute should always conduct detailed site investigations with 
careful monitoring, collective working and effective coordination among all the stakeholders. 
The poor performance of the project consultants and the contractors has been cited as the 
most common reason behind the inordinate delay. The Committee is of the view that the 

70 
 
Ministry could have explored the idea of appointing a single agency for architectural 
design/project consultancy and construction. The Committee also understands that employi ng 
a single agency may raise concern about the flaws in any phase of the project being ignored. 
Therefore, the Committee recommends that the Ministry should make a detailed assessment 
of the consultants/contractors and employ a trusted company with a prove n track record of 
executing such infrastructural projects. The Committee also believes that designing and 
planning is fundamental for the optimum utilisation of the space available. The Ministry 
should learn from the architectural /design failures in AIIMS  Rishikesh and ensure that such 
mistakes are avoided in the construction of other AIIMS. 
  (Para 9.13 of the Report) 
 
Action Taken 
 
1.55.6 Observations / Recommendations of the Committee have been noted for  future 
guidance. In the light of the experience g ained with the first six AIIMS projects, the new 
AIIMS works are now being assigned to empanelled PSUs having experience of building 
large hospitals and other building infrastructure. The PSUs are appointed as Executing 
Agencies (EAs) with full empowerment to take decisions on all contractual matters including 
power to award construction tenders. The Executing Agencies are also entrusted with the 
responsibility of appointment of design consultants, finalization of detailed designs and 
handling all coordination issues between the design consultants and the construction agencies. 
Thus, the work assigned to the Executing Agency is in the nature of turnkey assignment, with 
full empowerment and accountability. With this arrangement, the contractual matters need not 
have to come to Ministry for decision thus simplifying the process and also avoiding delays. 
The above system of assigning of project execution as a turnkey work to the EA has been 
introduced in 2015 onwards other than first six AIIMS are being executed  as per the new 
system. 
 
1.55.7 The new AIIMS works system has been introduced from 2016 at AIIMS Gorakhpur, 
Bhatinda and Guwahati and also taken up by the Executing Agencies in ―Engineering, 
Procurement and Construction (EPC)‖ mode.  AIIMS Deogarh and Bil aspurhave also been 
taken up in EPC mode by the Executing Agencies. In the EPC system, the construction 
agency itself is responsible for detailed designing and engineering and thus the problems of 
coordination between the Design consultant and the Construc tion agency and consequent risk 
of delays would get systemically addressed.  The Ministry of Health and Family Welfare do 
not have any departmental Civil, Electrical etc. wing with necessary expertise to assess the 
performance of Consultants / Contractors. However, Third Party Evaluation has been 
involved since Phase III of PMSSY which takes care of the systemic and structural issues 
encountered during evaluation of the project. The introduction of Panel of PSU‘s having good 
experience of construction of exe cution of large Hospital Projects shall mitigate any 
bottlenecks and drawbacks. 
 
 
 
 

71 
 
Recommendations/Observations  
 
1.55.8 The Committee is concerned with the manner in which arbitration cases have been 
dealt resulting in grant of compensations to contracto rs. The Committee recommends for an 
arbitration mechanism/cell to be institutionalized in the Ministry that would deal with all the 
arbitration cases and ensure speedy resolution. The Committee is concerned with arbitration 
issues arising in case of reputed companies like L&T and would like to be apprised of all such 
cases. The Committee recommends that a report on the status of all arbitration cases with 
respect to projects under PMSSY be provided to it so that a fair assessment of the impact of 
arbitration cases can be done. 
  (Para 9.15 of the Report) 
Action Taken 
 
1.55.9 The details of Arbitration cases arising out of implementation of six AIIMS of 
PMSSY are given in Annexure - Arbitration. As per Revised Cost Estimates, 2010 for setting 
up of six AIIMS,  the sanctioned amount by CCEA for capital construction was around Rs. 
3566.83 cr. Consultancy fee was pegged at Rs. 25 cr per Institute. Around 15 cases of dispute 
requiring Arbitration came out during implementation of the Scheme. In some of the cases 
where arbitration was completed, the Ministry / AIIMS paid around Rs. 14.7 cr to the 
contractors / Project Consultants. Rest of the awards have been challenged in court. Hence, a 
definitive figure on awarded cost can‘t be arrived at this point of time.  In six AIIMS already 
there is a team of engineers with one Superintending Engineer and it has been considered to 
deal with arbitration methods of the institutes. 
 
1.56 PROCUREMENT OF MEDICAL DEVICES AND EQUIPMENT 
 
Recommendations/Observations  
 
1.56.1 The Committee notes that the Procurement Support Agency (PSA) failed miserably in 
its task to ensure timely delivery and installation of the medical equipment. Reasons cited for 
non-installation of the medical equipment clearly highlight the fact that the PSA faile d to 
coordinate with the Institute that led to unnecessary delays. The Committee is of the opinion 
that the PSA in collaboration with the Institute should have resolved the user requirement, site 
details and other specific requirements with respect to the Medical Equipment well in 
advance. The Committee is highly disappointed by the performance of PSA and recommends 
for a strong monitoring of the function of PSA along with quarterly submission of a status 
Report on the procurement of medical devices/equipment to the Committee under the PMSSY 
scheme. 
  (Para 9.21 of the Report) 
Action Taken 
 
1.56.2 Observations / Recommendations of the Committee have been noted for future 
guidance. The deficiency as highlighted by the Hon‘ble Committee had been noted during 
implementation of the project. Hence in subsequent phase i.e. Phase  III of PMSSY, the Roles 
and Responsibilities of the Procurement Support Agency has been clearly demarcated in the 

72 
 
Procurement Policy of the PMSSY Division. Not only the role of the Procurem ent Agency 
but also the Roles and Responsibilities of the Executing Agency and respective Govt. Medical 
College under upgradation have also been spelt out. The quarterly status report on the 
procurement of medical devices/equipment to the Committee under t he PMSSY scheme will 
be submitted by PSA regularly. First Report from PSA (HITES) submitted. 
 
Recommendations/Observations  
 
1.56.3 The Committee notes that several medical devices and other equipment have been 
procured on single bid basis in all the AIIMS . In cases where multiple bids were allowed, 
many companies were rejected on technical grounds. The Institutes have insisted on US FDA 
certification even for basic equipment for which BIS/CE certified equipment of equivalent 
quality were available in the m arket. Superfluous expenditure was done to purchase beds, 
cushions, LED TV etc with US FDA certification. 
(Para 9.22 of the Report) 
 
1.56.4 The Committee strongly underlines the need to follow a competitive tendering 
mechanism for procurement of any equipm ent rather than a single bid tender procurement. 
Downright rejection on technical grounds should be avoided and any such rejection, if made, 
should always be substantiated by a detailed evaluation. The requirement of US FDA 
certification for even some of t he basic equipment is not only a burden on the exchequer but 
is also an outright contempt of the Make in India campaign. The Committee, therefore, 
strongly recommends that procurement of equipment/medical devices by the Institutes and the 
procurement agencies should be determined by quality, price and fair trade practice. 
(Para 9.23 of the Report) 
 
1.56.5 The Committee again emphasizes that non -transparent methods in tendering and 
award of contracts raises high chances of corruption, irregularities, non -value addition and 
encourage restrictive trade practices. Fair and equal opportunity to all the tenders promotes 
healthy competition and also offers the best price to the procurement agency. 
  (Para 9.24 of the Report) 
Action Taken 
 
1.56.6 Observations / Recommendations of the Committee have been noted for future 
compliance. Drugs Regulation Se ction of the Ministry issued guidelines regarding 
requirement / non -requirement of USFDA / CE certification, etc. in procurement of medical 
devices / equipments dated 20 .02.2018 which has been circulated by the PMSSY Division to 
HLL / HITES (Procurement Support Agency) / HSCC and all functioning AIIMS. PSA has 
intimated that they are allowing the standard of BIS or European CE or US FDA for PMSSY 
Procurement. In addition to above, in the MoA with the Procurement Support Agency (PSA), 
it has been stipulated that PSA will facilitate multi -vendor participation and procurement of 
Made in India medical equipments. Respective AIIMS have also been advised to follow the 
same. The Procurement Policy adopted by the PMSSY Division since March 2017 also 
clearly spells out the same.  
 

73 
 
Recommendations/Observations  
 
1.56.7 Attention of the Committee has also been drawn to the multiple rejections of bids on 
technical grounds and unnecessa ry delays caused during finalizing the technical specification 
of the equipment by individual AIIMS. In this regard, the Committee appreciates the 
suggestion of AIIMS Bhubaneswar and strongly recommends finalizing uniform technical 
specifications for equip ment especially high end/sophisticated equipment for all the AIIMS. 
The technical specifications of all the equipment should be finalized by a team of technical 
experts only after a detailed and scientific assessment. The Committee strongly recommends 
that the Ministry/Institute should give serious consideration to the suggestions of AIIMS 
Bhubaneswar for streamlining the procurement process. 
(Para 9.25 of the Report) 
 
1.56.8 The Committee further recommends that the other AIIMS like Institutions should 
emulate AIIMS Bhubaneswar that has procured 130 medical equipments with ISO/BIS 
certification. This would not only minimize the cost but also promote the domestic 
manufacturers thus boosting Government's flagship Make in India campaign. 
(Para 9.26 of the Report) 
Action Taken 
 
1.56.9 Observations / Recommendations of the Committee have been noted for future  
guidance. The procurement process is being further streamlined in view of various 
suggestions and recommendations of the Committee. A Team of technical expe rts is involved 
in framing the technical specification. In cases of insufficient competition, the technical 
specifications are revisited by the technical experts and tender re -invited as required. The 
issue of streamlining of procurement of medical equipme nt was discussed in the 3 rd Central 
Institute Body (CIB) meeting. It has been decided that various high value equipment would be 
procured centrally by AIIMS, New Delhi . In these procurements, the technical specification s 
are being finalized by a team of te chnical experts taken from INIs including AIIMS and other 
premier Central Institutes. Necessary instructions in this regard have been issued by the 
Ministry vide letter No- Z.28016/26/2018-PMSSY-I dated 29thJuly, 2019. 
 
Recommendations/Observations  
 
1.56.10 The Committee also appreciates the guidelines regarding requirement/non -
requirement of US FDA /CE certification etc. in the procurement of medical devices. The 
Para 2(i) of the guideline clearly states that wherever the Indian Standards are available th e 
Hospitals/Health Institutes/Organizations shall not insist on USFDA or CE certification. The 
Committee is concerned regarding the misuse of Para 2(ii) of these guidelines that gives an 
opportunity to the indenting organization to purchase medical equipme nt with USFDA 
certification in the name of quality. 
(Para 9.27 of the Report) 
Action Taken 
 
1.56.11 Observations / Recommendations of the Committee have been noted for future  
compliance. Every possible step is being taken to minimize procurement of US FDA / 

74 
 
European CE marked equipments by the Ministry / AIIMS and promote Make in India 
medical equipments. For procurement of medical equipments under PMSSY, the 
specifications are finalized by a group of specialist doctors (technical experts). The matter 
being of technical nature, the Ministry has to go by the advice of technical experts. 
 
Recommendations/Observations  
 
1.56.12 The Committee notes that even simple devices are being procured with 
USFDA mandatory certification in the backdrop of the discretionary  element in the 
guidelines. The Committee terms such provision as just another attempt to camouflage the 
USFDA requirement in the name of quality which is a setback to competitive pricing. The 
Committee, therefore, strongly recommends that the Ministry sho uld fix certification 
requirement as USFDA or CE or Bureau of Indian Standards (BIS) or any other 
internationally accepted certification in the interim till BIS standards are established 
universally so as to leave no option for discretion and its misuse. B IS through its Medical 
Devices Management System Certification Scheme IS/ISO 13485 gives certification to the 
medical equipment which are Made in India. The Committee is of the view that procurement 
of indigenously produced medical equipment will promote a nd encourage companies to 
manufacture their products under the "Make in India" scheme creating thereby additional 
employment opportunities and empowering secondary and tertiary sector of the country. The 
Committee, therefore, recommends that spirit of the guideline under reference must be 
executed on the ground level to encourage flagship ―Make in India‖ programme. The matter 
may also be taken up on priority basis; with the BIS so that every single equipment procured 
in India has a BIS standard which would be equivalent to international quality certification. 
 
(Para 9.28 of the Report) 
Action Taken 
 
1.56.13 The Ministry has already initiated action in this regard. Secretary, Ministry of 
Health and Family Welfare vide letter dated 7 thMay, 2018 to Director Gen eral, Bureau of 
Indian Standards requesting formulation of specifications for all the commonly procured 
medical equipments to promote ‗Make in India‘ policy of the Government of India. Bureau of 
Indian Standards has already started creation of BIS standard s for Medical Equipments and 
Devices. As per information available with the Ministry around standardization list for around 
1000 medical devices have almost been completed. Apart from the above items, HITES has 
submitted list of commonly procured medical e quipment numbering 454 items to BIS for 
standardization. BIS then formed a Core committee for formulating Indian Standard in 2018. 
The committee comprises of members from BIS, NHSRC, AMTZ, and Industry bodies like 
FICCI, Assocham, CII, AiMED, USIBC, MTaI a nd Domestic & Foreign manufacturers. 
Among these 454 items, BIS standards of around 276 items were finalized and published in 
2019. Remaining items are under process.  
 
 
 
 

75 
 
Recommendations/Observations  
 
1.56.14 The Committee has also come across several di screpancies in the procurement 
exercise. Despite similar CMC/training/warranty conditions, a substantial price differential 
has been observed in the procurement of the same medical equipment by the private corporate 
hospitals and the new AIIMS. The Committ ee is concerned to note that AIIMS like 
Institutions have purchased the same medical equipment at a higher cost than their 
counterparts in the health industry. The Committee recommends that the Ministry should 
carry out a detailed assessment in all such ca ses where price differential is more than 10% in 
the procurement of equipment by private/corporate hospitals and AIIMS like Institutions. 
(Para 9.29 of the Report) 
 
Action Taken  
 
1.56.15 Observations / Recommendations of the Committee have been noted for future 
compliance. It is submitted that HLL/ HITES (PSA) invites open tender through global tender 
mechanism. Since HLL is into the field of medical equipment procurement since last 8 -9 
years we are having our own data base. PSA also compare prices with or ders placed by other 
reputed institute like AIIMS New Delhi, PGI Chandigarh, JIMPER, NIMHANS etc. PSA also 
compare rates with big corporate Hospitals like Apollo, Max, Fortis, Sri  Gangaram Hospital 
wherever the rates of such equipment are available. Howeve r HLL/ HITES stated that with 
conditions like equipment are brand new, unused, latest launch, all necessary updates are free 
during warranty and CMC period our prices are much cheaper than the other corporate and 
Govt. Orders.  
 
Recommendations/Observations

Recommendations/Observations  
 
1.56.19 The Committee takes a serious view of the observations of CAG with respect 
to the performance of the PSA and would like to be apprised of the time bound corrective 
actions taken on the CAG observations so that the procurement process can be streamlined. 
 
(Para 9.31 of the Report) 
 
Action Taken  
 
1.56.20 In this connection, the follo wing points/facts are submitted. [An agreement has 
been entered into betwee n MoH&FW and HITES on 6 th March, 2019 for procurement of 
medical equipment for AIIMS under Phase -IV & V of PMSSY. As per ―Clause III of 
Appendix C‖ of the Contract Agreement dated 06.03.2019, signed between this Ministry and 
M/s HITES for procurement of me dical equipment for new AIIMS under Phase -IV &V of 
PMSSY, 100% procurement cost should be released to the PSA before placement of 
Notification of Award to Suppliers.  An Addendum was signed on 25.09.2024 which provides 
that 100% of procurement cost shall be released before placement of Notification of Award in 
r/o items which require opening of Line of Credit (LC) in financial institution(s) by the 'PSA; 
and for other items which does not require opening of Line of Credit (LC) in financial 
institution(s) by the 'PSA', the client reserves the right to release funds in suitable tranches. It 
was further clarified that:- 
 
(a) 25% of the equipment cost will be provided as an advance before the issuance of the 
Notification of Award (NoA). 
 
(b) After delivery of the equipme nt, HITES can further claim 50% of the cost after 
furnishing the Consignee Receipt Certificate (CRC). 
 
(c) The remaining 25% can be claimed upon receipt of the Final Acceptance Certificate 
(FAC). 
 
 

78 
 
1.57 QUALITY OF MEDICAL EDUCATION 
Recommendations/Observations  
 
1.57.1 The Committee observes that providing quality education and producing better trained 
doctors is a major challenge for the new AIIMS like Institutions. The Committee also notes 
that some of the new AIIMS have started Post Graduate courses in diffe rent disciplines. 
Attention of the Committee has been drawn to the fact that the Post Graduates from these 
Institutes lack the required clinical exposure and practical training which should have been 
integral for all Graduates/Post Graduates in the AIIMS. 
(Para 9.37 of the Report) 
 
1.57.2 The Committee is of the considered view that starting PG courses is irrelevant until 
and unless the Institute is able to provide quality training under the supervision of full time 
expert faculties. The Committee strongly emphasizes the importance of proper clinical 
training of the postgraduate students. The Committee further recommends that the AIIMS like 
Institutions should emphasize on the applied aspects of various disciplines through practical 
training. AIIMS like Inst itutions should be the frontrunner in filling the critical gaps in the 
health sector by producing well trained specialists in different disciplines. 
(Para 9.38 of the Report) 
 
1.57.3 The Committee is also concerned to note that most of the AIIMS do not hav e any 
faculty at the HOD/Professor level from AIIMS Delhi or PGI or JIPMER or any premier 
National Institute. The experience and expertise of faculty/HOD has an impact on the quality 
of Medical Graduates. In most of the AIIMS, the faculty lacks any teachin g experience from 
any of the premier Institutes. This raises serious concern about the dilution of the AIIMS 
Delhi benchmark. The Committee would also like to be apprised of the number of clinical 
cases, discipline wise that have been dealt by the PG students from various Departments. 
(Para 9.39 of the Report) 
 
Action Taken  
 
1.57.4 The concerns of the esteemed Committee are noted for future guidance. The Institutes 
have also been sensitized in this matter.  The matter  of starting new PG courses are first 
deliberated in the Academic Committee meeting and courses are added after ensuring that HR 
and facilities are available to provide sufficient clinical exposure and practical training to the 
PG students.  Most of the faculty in all AIIMS has been trained in I NIs and other reputed 
Institutes at the level of PG/SR/Faculty. Many of the existing HODs have worked in AIIMS, 
New Delhi/PGIMER/JIPMER/SGPGI prior to joining at new AIIMS. All faculties have the 
prescribed teaching/research experience and most of them hav e trained from INIs.  The 
faculty members are being selected by the Standing Selection Committee purely on the basis 
of merit maintaining the standards and all faculties have had experience of teaching, research 
and patient care. They train the PGs in accordance with established curricula. 
 

79 
 
1.57.5 AIIMS undertakes their responsibility of training of Post Graduate Students very 
seriously and provide therewith a structural training program in all Departments. Students 
have adequate clinical exposure in all cl inical departments with a wide variety of cases and 
diseases being treated. PG training program is carried out by each department through various 
modalities like Seminars, Journal Clubs, Interactive sessions, Case/Practicals/Community 
discussions. Post graduate students are assessed regularly through formative assessments. Post 
graduate students are involved in all activities of their departments including both teaching, 
research and patient care. The post graduate students are associated with all the clini cal cases 
both in OPD, IPDs, Operation Theatres, both in Surgical and Medical departments. As the 
patient footfall is high, a large number and variety of cases come to these AIIMS ensuring 
adequate clinical exposure of all PGs.  Efforts are made to creating  AIIMS like ―Culture of 
Excellence‖ in new AIIMS and a Visiting Committee under chair of Dr VK Paul constituted 
to identify gaps and help institutionalize systems for training, handholding and Faculty 
exchange programmes. 
 
 
1.58 LOW BED OCCUPANCY RATE 
 
Recommendations/Observations  
 
1.58.1 Attention of the Committee has been drawn to the low bed occupancy in the AIIMS 
like Institutions. In case of AIIMS Bhopal, the bed occupancy rate is just 10% in the 
Ophthalmology Department against 16 beds. The Committee  is surprised to note that even in 
an important Department like Obstetrics and Gynecology, the bed occupancy is just 37% 
against 40 beds. 
(Para 9.40 of the Report) 
 
1.58.2 The Committee is of the opinion that the AIIMS like Institutions that were anticipat ed 
to serve as an excellent centre of medical care have failed miserably to accomplish the desired 
objective. The low bed occupancy rate speaks volumes about the prevalent distrust in the 
healthcare facilities of the new AIIMS. The Committee is astonished to note that even in 
Obstetrics and Gynecology Department, only 37% bed occupancy exist. 
(Para 9.41 of the Report) 
 
1.58.3 The Committee is coerced to draw comparison with a hitherto neglected institute i.e. 
Regional Institute of Medical Sciences (RIMS) Im phal where the Committee witnessed for 
itself during the course of its study visit that the bed occupancy rate is 100 % in RIMS 
Imphal. The Committee also noted that there were 210 beds in Department of Obstetrics and 
Gynecology with 100% occupancy through out the year and RIMS carried out 11,000 
deliveries per year. The Committee would appreciate if the new AIIMS like Institutions match 
at least 25% of the performance delivery of RIMS Imphal which the Committee witnessed for 
itself during the study visit of the Committee. 
(Para 9.42 of the Report) 
 
 
 

80 
 
Action Taken

Rishikesh 88.19

1.59 GOVERNING BODY (GB) 
Recommendations/Observations  
 
1.59.1 The Committee strongly believes regular review and progress meetings instills a sense 
of accountability in the Institute and this system of check and balance is very important for 
smooth running of the Institute. The Committee also recommends that all major decisions by 
the Director and the President of the Regional AIIMS, should be approved by the IB/GB and 
any new project should be taken up after due approval of the IB/GB. The Committee also 
recommends that no ex -post-facto approval should be given under any circumstances. The 
Ministry should explore the possibility to assign overall supervisory role to CIB, while 
keeping the functional autonomy of each AIIMS intact. The Committee also recommends th at 
the vacancy in respect of Members of Parliament in the Governing Body of all new AIIMS 
must be filled up without further delay. This will not only ensure representation of the people 
in the Governing body but will also result in its better functioning in a democratic manner. 
 
(Para 9.46 of the Report) 
Action Taken  
 
1.59.2 Observations / Recommendations of the Committee have been noted for  future 
compliance. The issues highlighted by the Committee have already been discussed in the first 
three meetings of CIB and approved. Vacancy in respect of MP‘s in the IB of all new AIIMS 

81 
 
have been filled up. The respective I B, have been advised to constitute the GB including the 
elected representative. A Central Institute Body has been created to take major policy decision 
common to all AIIMS and to review of the financial, physical and recruitment progress and 
over all progress of the new AIIMS.  Vacancies in respect of MPs in the IBs of new AIIMS 
have been filled up.  The respective IBs have been advised to constitu te the GBs including the 
elected representative. 
 
Recommendations/Observations  
 
1.59.3 The Committee also welcomes the recent process of decentralization in all the six 
AIIMS with the appointment of their respective Presidents which would entail greater 
accountability and better monitoring. The constitution of a Central Institute Body (CIB) is 
also a step in the right direction, as this would facilitate the evolution of common standards 
across all AIIMS on all important benchmarks like human resources, pro curement, expansion 
and project execution. The Committee would also like to appreciate the increasing receptivity 
and meticulous approach of the Ministry in taking corrective measures in rectifying the flaws 
in the execution of PMSSY scheme and hopes that objective of the PMSSY scheme i.e. to 
provide tertiary health care facilities in the remotest regions of the country is realized. 
 
(Para 9.47 of the Report)

1.61 DIALYSIS FACILITIES

1.61.1 The Committee notes that in AIIMS Bhopal, only three out of sanctioned eight 
dialysis machines are functional; in AIIMS Bhubaneswar; setting up of a dialysis unit with 14 
dialysis machines is still under process; in AIIMS Jodhpur, 11 dialysis machines are 
functional; in AIIMS Rishikesh 3 dialysis machines are functional; in AIIMS Patna and 
AIIMS Raipur, the Department of Nephrology is not yet functional. 
(Para 9.53 of the report) 
 
1.61.2 The Committee, therefore, strongly recommends that all new AIIMS should establish 
a fully equipped Nephrology Department immediately. In view of the rising demand of 
dialysis services, a fully functional Nephrology De partment is critical in providing tertiary 
health care facilities. The Committee also recommends that all AIIMS like Institutions should 
ensure availability of adequate number of functional dialysis machines and skilled manpower 
in the Institute. 
(Para 9.54 of the report)

1.62 HIGHER EDUCATION FINANCING AGENCY (HEFA) 
Recommendations/Observations  
 
1.62.1 The Committee is aware that raising funds from the market through HEFA is a 
herculean tas k and there would be a requirement of pledging of Government resources as 
collateral to HEFA to obtain loans. It has also been further mentioned that these loans would 
be serviced through internal accruals. The Committee is concerned to note that non -judicious 
usage of loans from HEFA by the regional AIIMS will create a vicious cycle wherein these 
AIIMS would be subject to a debt trap and would again seek budgetary support to service the 
loans obtained from HEFA. The Committee is of the considered opinion t hat any loan 

84 
 
proposal sent to HEFA should be duly vetted by the CIB after obtaining a comparative 
assessment from all the six AIIMS regarding the sourcing of funds and the ability of AIIMS 
to service the funds. The Committee foresees a situation where reck less expenditure from the 
loans obtained from HEFA may result in a situation where the incumbent Directors of the 
regional AIIMS may retire but the new incumbent would be faced with a fresh challenge of 
servicing the loans obtained from HEFA. 
(Para 9.57 of the Report) 
Action Taken  
 
1.62.2 The Ministry is providing funds to the respective AIIMS through Grants -in-Aid 
(General) which is provided by the Ministry of Finance through Budgetary grants for 
servicing of the loan from HEFA. Respective AIIMS do not ha ve any obligation to service 
loans from HEFA through internal revenue generation. There will be no liability for the 
respective AIIMS as far as HEFA loan is concerned. Taking note of the concern raised by the 
Hon‘ble DRPSC, the CIB has duly considered the proposal/demands of the functional AIIMS 
for non- DPR works and allow availing of HEFA loans by these AIIMS keeping in view the 
long terms loan servicing obligations of the HEFA loans. 
 
Recommendations/Observations  
 
1.62.3 The Committee would like to be u pdated on a quarterly basis regarding all 
loan/advances obtained from HEFA alongwith the measures/proposals under consideration 
towards timely repayment of these loans. The Committee also recommends that 
accountability of these loans be clearly fixed so th at there are no legal issues in this regard in 
future. The Committee also recommends tapping donations and CSR funds as has been done 
by AIIMS Bhubaneswar and AIIMS Patna for projects like Dharmshala, etc. by the regional 
AIIMS. 
(Para 9.58 of the Report) 
Action Taken  
 
1.62.4 Observations / Recommendations of the Committee have been noted for future  
guidance.  
1.63 SPECIALITY/SUPER SPECIALITY DEPARTMENTS AND ADVANCE 
CENTRES 
Recommendations/Observations  
 
1.63.1 The Committee appreciates the good intention b ehind construction of such 
Departments but after a broad study of all the AIIMS like Institutes, the Committee realizes 
that there is an urgent need to scrutinize the proper functioning of such Departments. In many 
AIIMS, the required infrastructure is pre sent but due to acute shortage of the medical 
faculty/specialist, the super -speciality departments are non -functional. This is a typical 
example of constructing buildings without foundation. With no staff, the infrastructure and 
the resources are lying untapped and unutilized. 
(Para 9.60 of the Report) 

85 
 
 
Action Taken  
 
1.63.2 Observations/Recommendations of the Committee have been noted for taking  
correcting measures. 
 
Recommendations/Observations  
 
1.63.3 Attention of the Committee has also been drawn to th e different proposals of AIIMS 
like Institutions for establishing Advance Ophthalmic Centre, Advance Cardiac Centre, 
Advance Neurosciences Centre, Advance Transplant Centre & Advance Oncology Centre. 
(Para 9.61 of the Report) 
 
1.63.4 The Committee is espec ially worried about the viability of such Advance Centres in 
these six AIIMS like Institutions that have a very low average daily OPD footfall, with the 
highest being 2,949 at AIIMS Bhubaneswar and the lowest being 1300 at AIIMS Raipur. The 
Committee canno t comprehend the need for establishing advance centre for Institutes with 
such low OPD footfall, especially when these Institutes have failed to make optimum 
utilization of the available resource and infrastructure. The Committee also emphasizes that 
many faculty posts are still lying vacant in various Departments and even Speciality and 
Super Speciality Departments are non -functional. In such scenario, these Institutes vying for 
Advance Centres only reflect a poor understanding of the existing grim reality. 
(Para 9.62 of the Report) 
 
1.63.5 The Committee strongly recommends that the Ministry/Institute should carry out a 
cost benefit analysis before any proposal for building an advance centre in the new AIIMS is 
passed. The Committee would like the Ministry/ Institute to furnish a status note and a cost 
benefit analysis on the proposed and sanctioned advance centers in all the AIIMS like 
Institutions. The Committee is of the view that the decisions regarding establishment of 
Advance Centers in all the new AIIM S should be taken by Central Institute Body. The 
Committee also strongly recommends that in the new AIIMS, an advance centre should only 
be established if the Institutions are able to achieve an OPD level which is 50% of the average 
daily OPD footfall of A IIMS Delhi. The utility of such advance centres has to be closely 
scrutinized so that the available resources are optimally put to use. 
 
(Para 9.63 of the Report) 
Action Taken  
 
1.63.6 Observations / Recommendations of the Hon‘ble Committee have been noted  for 
future guidance. Each proposal for Advanced Centre is examined in the SFC and GB meeting 
of the respective Institutes. The Institutes have been sensitized about the 
observations/recommendations of the Committee. Further, all investment proposals above  
Rs.100 crores are also brought before the Central Institute Body  (CIB). However, as advised 
by the Committee, the various proposals regarding establishment of Advanced Centres will be 
brought before the CIB for consideration, approval.  

86 
 
 
1.63.7 It is, how ever mentioned there that AIIMS, Delhi has attained its current Status of 
Excellency in more than six decades after its establishment. The size of AIIMS, Delhi  in 
respect of bad capacity and number of faculty is more than three times of the size of six ne w 
AIIMS. In view of this, the new Institutes may not able to achieve the specified 50% of 
average of daily OPD footfall of AIIMS, Delhi even on attaining almost 100% capacity 
utilization. The need for augmenting facilities and setting up of Advanced Centre s to provide 
advance tertiary health care, medical education and research facilities in these new AIIMS 
which are established in different regions of the country, would be expected to arise sooner as 
the Institute attained full 960 bed capacity and availability of 70-80% of faculty. However, the 
observations and concern made by the Hon‘ble Committee have been noted and due care will 
be taken to set up Advanced Centres only after the Institutes have attained certain level of 
maturity in terms of capacity utilization and availability of human resource.

1.64 OPD FOOTFALL

be avoided. The Committee strongly recommends that all  the patients should be given a 
unique ID that should form the basis of measuring patient load in an Institute. The possibility 
to exaggerate OPD footfall/number of procedures (major and minor surgeries) when the same 
patient visits multiple Departments in  a day should be avoided. The Committee strongly 
recommends all the Institutes to measure accurate OPD footfall/procedures and avert from 
extrapolating the OPD/surgery numbers. The Committee strongly advocates strengthening the 
system of online registratio n for all the AIIMS like Institutions and work towards effective 
implementation of Hospital Management Information System. 
(Para 9.65 of the Report)

guidance. Each Institute has been advised to implement a hospital information system with 

87

implemented in AIIMS, Delhi, has also been adopted and implemented in all the six AIIMS.  
 
Recommendations/Observations  
 
1.64.3 The Committee would also like to be apprised whether the establishment of AIIMS in 
the six States has had any impact on the patient load in AIIMS Delhi from these States. The 
idea behind regional AIIMS was to provide  tertiary health care facilities in all the States so 
that the patients from these States do not have to rush to AIIMS Delhi for even basic medical 
care. Therefore, the Committee desires AIIMS Delhi to submit a report on the impact these 
new AIIMS like Institutions had on patient load. AIIMS Delhi should also submit a report on 
the number of Patients that were transferred from the regional AIIMS to AIIMS Delhi.

1.65 OPERATION THEATRES AND SURGERY ISSUES  
Recommendations/Observations  
 
1.65.1 The Committee strongly recommends all the AIIMS like Institute to expedite the 
construction of the Operation Theatres and provid e the required infrastructure. The 
Committee also recommends all the AIIMS like Institutes to carry out a detailed analysis of 
the operation theatre utilization at their respective Institutes and submit the same to the 
Committee. The Committee also believe s that there is an urgent need to follow strict 
Operation Theatre guidelines so that OTs start on time and the time between two surgeries in 
a single day is optimally utilized and not wasted. Better planning of operation schedule and 
timings can definitely increase the efficiency of OTs. The Committee also takes a strong note 
of the existing large discrepancies in the data provided by each Institute. The Committee 
strongly recommends following a uniform criteria for classification of surgeries as minor and 
major so that accurate data related to the number of surgeries conducted can be obtained. 
 
(Para 9.68 of the Report) 
 

88

schedules and timings has been noted. All AIIMS have been advised for optimal utilization of 
the scarce resource like operation theatre time.  The Institutes have also been advised to

'The Audit Report of the CAG (Report No.19 of 2013 - Union Government -Civil) 
contained observations relating to irregularities in selection of project consultants, irregular 
expenditure on escalation charges amounting to Rs. 1.56 crore, in correct release of 
mobilization advance amounting to Rs. 8.32 crore to contractors, excess payment of Rs.25.20

fund requirements instead of on actual progress of work.  In reply to the audit comments, the

(Para 9.69 of the Report) 
 
1.65.5 The CAG Report on PMSSY has highlighted various inadequacies and problem areas 
in setting up of new AIIMS and upgradation programme of Government Medical Colleges. 
The CAG Report No . 10 contains observations relating to cost and time overrun, excess

shortage of faculty and non -faculty posts, non -functional speciality and super speciality

Action Taken  
 
1.65.6 The position on these observations has been adequately explained in Ministry‘s 
detailed reply. It is further stated that it is not a correct appreciation on the part of the CAG 
that irregularities of similar nature have occurred. The position on these obs ervations of CAG 
has been explained fully in Ministry‘s response to CAG . Institutes are taking necessary action 
to ensure that new construction projects are completed within the stipulated period of the 
contract. Similarly, procurement of medical equipment  are also processed where site for 
installation and faculty to operate the equipment are available.  It is stated that the several 
steps/policy interventions have been taken by the Government to improve the faculty position 
so as to ensure full functionality of all Departments under the six new AIIMS. In addition, the 
Ministry has also taken following policy initiatives for bringing about improved availability 
of trained HR at National Level at large. 
 
1.65.7 A Central Institute Body has been created and emp owered to take common policy 
decisions concerning all AIIMS to bring uniformity in policies across all AIIMS and to 
facilitate effective Governance of the AIIMS.  All AIIMS have been advised to conduct 
recruitments for Non -faculty (Technical) cadres on cent ralized basis. It has also mentioned 
here that presently the construction is being taken up in the EPC mode wherein extensive 
problem of large deviation / escalation would get systematically obviated. Further, the system 
of assigning the construction work to executing agencies on turnkey basis with empowerment 
to handle all contractual matters at their end has been introduced.  The executing agencies 
have been mandated to deploy and use Project Management Software to bring about 
improved project management at their end. The Ministry has also developed an online 
dashboard to facilitate a closer and more effective monitoring. The functional AIIMS have 
been empowered with greater delegation of powers in construction, procurement upto  Rs. 5 
crore in cases and al so in administrative matters. The procedure for procurement of medical 
equipment has been simplified and made more efficient with greater delegation and 
empowerment of the Procurement Support Agency (PSA) which is entrusted with the work of 

90 
 
procurement of medical equipments. All new AIIMS are now being designed keeping in view 
the ―GRIHA‖ norms for green buildings. 
 
Recommendations/Observations  
 
1.65.8 The Committee besides recommending for decentralization of healthcare facilities in 
the most remote areas  of the country is inclined to emphasize that central Government and 
State Government must make policy and chalk out strategy and formulate/appropriate course 
of action for delivering Health Care Services at the Doorstep with the purpose of making 
Ayushman Bharat a successful scheme. 
(Para 9.71 of the Report) 
 
Action Taken  
 
1.65.9 Ayushman Bharat Pradhan Mantri - Jan ArogyaYojana (AB PM -JAY) is a flagship 
scheme of the Government which provides Rs 5 lakh health cover per beneficiary family 
annually for sec ondary and tertiary care to approx. 55 crore beneficiaries (12.37 crore 
economically vulnerable families). Recently, it expanded to cover approx. 6 crore senior 
citizens (70+) from 4.5 crore families, regardless of socio -economic status. Under Ayushman 
Bharat Pradhan Mantri Jan ArogyaYojana (AB PM -JAY) initially, 10.74 Crore beneficiary 
families under AB PM -JAY were targeted on the basis of the Socio -economic Caste Census 
(SECC) of 2011 using select deprivation and occupational criteria across rural and urb an 
areas respectively to identify the families. Further, in January 2022, on the basis of decadal 
growth rate of 11.7%, the Government of India, revised the beneficiary base to 12 Crore 
families. 
 
1.65.10 Further, in April 2022, as per the approval of Govt  of India, NHA had 
provided the flexibility to States/UTs to use non -SECC Aadhaar verified databases of similar 
socio-economic profile for verification of beneficiaries against such SECC beneficiaries who 
couldn't be identified and verified. Accordingly, m any States have used databases of poor and 
vulnerable families against unidentified SECC families. (including National Food Security 
Act, State specific datasets, etc).  In March 2024, 37 lakh families of ASHA, Anganwadi 
Worker and Anganwadi Helpers were al so included in the scheme.  Further, on 29th October 
2024, the Government expanded AB PM -JAY to provide free treatment benefits of up to ₹5 
lakh per year to all senior citizens aged 70 years and above, irrespective of their socio -
economic status. This aim to benefit approx. 6 crore s enior citizens from 4.5 crore families 
across the country.  In addition, many States/UTs implementing AB PM -JAY have expanded 
the beneficiary base under the scheme, at their own cost. 
 
1.65.11 Overall Scheme Progress 
 Scheme has been implemented in all the States/UTs except West Bengal 
 Ayushman Cards Created: As on 01st April 2025, more than 40 Crore Ayushman 
cards have been created. 
 Hospitals Empanelled: A total of 31,005 hospitals are empanelled under the scheme 
out of which 13,879 empanelled hospitals are private and the rest are public. 

91 
 
 Hospital Admissions: More than 9.19 Crore Hospital Admissions worth over Rs. 1.29 
Crore have been undertaken in the scheme since its inception. 
 Fund Released to States:  In FY 2024 -25, approx. 6637 Cr have been released to 
States/UTs.

RECOMMENDATIONS/OBSERVATIONS ON WHICH THE COMMITTEE DOES

A. AIIMS BHOPAL 
 
2.1 Budgetary Allocation 
 
Recommendations/Observations 
 
2.1.1 The Committee notes with concern the observations of CAG that only 50% 
(approximately) of the funds allocated for AIIMS, Bhopal had been utilized between 2011 
and 2017. The Committee is of the firm view that the budgetary allocation should have been 
properly utilized under a strategic plan of action to prevent underutilization. The Committee 
observes the increasing trend of expenditure since 2017 -18 when the expansion work got 
momentum. The Committee also observes that allocation of funds to the tune of Rs.  99.90 
crore in the financial year 2017-18 was less than the actual expenditure of Rs. 237.24 crore in 
the said year and the remaining expenditure was met with unspent balances of the prior 
period. 
(Para 2.6 of the Report) 
Action Taken          
 
2.1.2 The substantial progress towards utilization of funds was made in the year 2017 -18. 
The unutilized balance was brought down to Rs.97.15 Crore as on March, 2018.  There was 
further improvement in the expenditure percentage and reduction in un -utilised fund duri ng 
2018-19 as brought out below: -

2.2 PROCUREMENT OF MEDICAL EQUIPMENT 
 
Recommendations/Observations  
 
2.2.1 The Committee understands that United States Food and Drug Administration 
(USFDA) is a regulatory mechanism/certification for protecting and promoting public health 
by way of control and supervision of food and drugs including medical devices with specif ic 
reference to the United States but there is hardly any country in the world besides the United 
States which makes a provision for allowing USFDA as a mandatory condition. The 
Committee fails to comprehend the reasons for procuring 50 equipment s with USF DA 
certification, some of which are easily available with CE marking and Bureau of Indian 
Standards (BIS) (CE/BIS) certification. The Committee, therefore, recommends to the 
Institute to procure equipment with CE/BIS certification so as to encourage Medica l Device 
Industry in India and participate in the Government's "Make in India" initiative. 
(Para 2.17 of the Report) 
 
Action Taken 
 
2.2.2 Drugs Regulation Section of the Ministry vide letter dated 20.02.2018 issued 
guidelines regarding requirement/ non -requirement of USFDA/ CE certification, etc. in 
procurement of medical devices/ equipment which was also circulated by the PMSSY 
Division to HLL/ HITES (Procurement Support Agency) / HSCC and all functioning AIIMS. 
PSA has intimated that besides USFDA they ar e also allowing option of BIS or European CE 
standard to the vendors as available, for PMSSY procurement . Procurement of Equipment in 
AIIMS, Bhopal is being done through their Procurement Support Agency (PSA) i.e. M/s 
HLL-HITES (PSA)/e-Tender/GeM in which following options are generally mentioned in the 
tenders for the purpose of wide range for economic selection of the equipment proposed to be 

95 
 
procured i.e. DCGI/CE (Europe)/USFDA/WHO/GMP/BIS/IS/CDSCO (whichever is 
applicable for medical devices /Equipment/ consumables). Besides above, adopting clause 
regarding purchase preference policy is also mentioned wherever possible to give thereby 
importance to ―Make in India‖ products. At present, the procurement of medical equipment at 
AIIMS Bhopal is carried out in  accordance with government guidelines through mandatory 
online platforms such as the GEM portal, CPP portal and procurement support agencies like 
HLL/HITES as per MOU.  The tender documents are designed to facilitate a broad  and cost-
effective selection of the equipment to be procured. 
 
Recommendations/Observations  
 
2.2.3 The Committee believes that the lackadaisical approach of the Institute has resulted in 
non-installation of the equipment which has delayed operationalization of the critical 
healthcare f acilities, thus, depriving the patients of key diagnostic and in -patient facilities. 
The Committee, therefore, recommends to the Institute to have a system in place for ensuring 
timely procurement as well as installation of the equipment so that the equipment are properly 
and optimally utilized for the purpose for which they were procured. The Committee also 
recommends for immediate installation of 21 types of equipment costing Rs. 27.9 crore that 
have been procured but not installed yet. The Committee furt her calls upon the Ministry to 
look into matter of procurement of 25 equipment costing Rs. 84.7 crore which are yet to be 
received and installed in AIIMS, Bhopal. In view of the delays in procurement and 
installation of equipment in the Institute, the Comm ittee impresses upon the Ministry to 
ensure effective monitoring through ABC analysis (Always a better control) by the Technical 
Specification Committees at the Ministry and institutional level to ensure much needed 
synchronization and coordination of acti vities with regard to procurement and installation of 
equipment. 
(Para 2.23 of the Report) 
Action Taken 
 
2.2.4 Presently, the construction of all infrastructure works has been completed. In the 
present system, there exist a check and balance to ensure time ly supply of equipment by 
forecasting the site readiness from the user end and other resources linked with installation of 
the equipment. Further, a suitable provision has been incorporated in tender document for 
work on turkey basis for smooth installatio n and commissioning of the equipment wherever 
applicable.  At present, AIIMS Bhopal does not have any equipment which is procurement 
and uninstalled. 
2.3 BLOOD BANK FACILITY 
 
Recommendations/Observations  
 
2.3.1 The Committee is disturbed to note the non -availability of blood bank facility at the 
Institute even after five years of functional IPD services. The Committee is of the view that 
blood bank services are an integral part of any hospital system. The Committee, therefore, 

96 
 
strongly recommends to the In stitute to expedite the setting up of fully functional blood bank 
facility without further delay. 
(Para 2.51 of the report) 
Action Taken 
2.3.2 The fully functional blood bank facility is available at AIIMS, Bhopal. 
2.4 ISSUE OF CONTRACTUAL APPOINTMENTS 
 
Recommendations/Observations  
 
2.4.1 The Committee was informed that the doctors of AYUSH Hospital have been working 
on contractual basis for the past several years. The Committee during its study visit found that 
the OPD footfall in the AYUSH centre was muc h higher despite limited infrastructure. The 
Committee appreciates the work ethics of the three AYUSH doctors in treating a large 
number of patients having faith in the Indian system of Medicine. The Committee feels that 
given the positive response towards  AYUSH services, the Ministry should contemplate of 
having a permanent staff instead of engaging contractual workforce. This would ensure job 
stability and security to the employees and in turn the organization would be benefitted from 
huge experience of t hese doctors gained during their service at AYUSH Hospital. The 
Committee, therefore, strongly recommends the Department to revisit the rules regarding 
contractual appointments so as to regularize the existing AYUSH doctors to permanent 
appointments in view of their dedicated service and outstanding performance. 
(Para 2.54 of the report) 
 
Action Taken 
 
2.4.2 Ministry has decided to integrate the AYUSH in the tertiary care system in new 
AIIMS by augmenting the strength of AYUSH professionals and by also crea ting scientific 
posts in order to undertake academic Ph.D programme and finding solutions to India‘s public 
health challenge. M/o AYUSH had advised that each of the new and upcoming AIIMS should 
have augmented faculty / doctor strength and higher focus in one particular traditional system 
of medicine which may be having popularity in the particular region. For example, 
AYURVEDA system of Indian medicine is recommended for AIIMS, Rishikesh, while 
SIDDHA is identified for upcoming AIIMS in Tamil Nadu, where S IDDHA is popular. Each 
such augmented medicine system will have additional 16 posts, consisting of posts of 
Professor, Additional Professor, Associate Professor, Assistant Professor, SR/JR posts. This 
will be over and above the already sanctioned five Medi cal Officer posts, one each in 
Ayurveda, Yoga and Naturopathy, Unani, Siddha and Homeopathy. The above structure of 
AYUSH Department has been included in the Standard Staffing Pattern approved by Central 
Institute Body (CIB). The proposal for creation of p osts is being taken up with Department of 
Expenditure accordingly. 
2.4.3 Institute has completed the recruitment of Medical Officers of AYUSH Department at 
AIIMS Bhopal against the Advertisement dated 20.07.2017.  In the light of the direction, of 

97 
 
the esteemed Committee, the Ministry has, ab -initio, considered the matter Regularisat ion of 
Contractual employees.  In this regard, a reference has been made to DoPT vide the 
Ministry‘s letter dated 28/9/2018 seeking their guidance as to what preferential treatmen t and 
any other benefits that can be extended in favour of the contractual staff in the matter of 
considering them for  regular appointment in AIIMS. Clarifications form DoPT has been 
received in the above matter. It has been advised that regularization of contractual employees 
is not permissible in the light of the Judgement pronounced by the Hon‘ble Supreme Court in 
OA No. 43/2018 in the case of Uma Devi. As regards the standard staffing pattern of AYUSH 
structure, the matter has already been taken up with  Department of Expenditure on 27 th May, 
2019 and the same is under process.  The following four Medical Officers have joined the 
AYUSH Department on regular basis as per details given below –  
  
1. Dr. BarkatiMohdTarique, MO (Unani)  w.e.f. 15.10.2018 
2. Dr.Ashish Kumar Dixit, MO (Homeopathy) w.e.f. 15.10.2018 
3. Dr.RanjanaPandey, MO (Ayurveda)  w.e.f. 02.11.2018 
4. Dr. Mudda Sofia, MO (Yoga) w.e.f. 01.04.2019 (after resignation of   
Dr.Mooventhan A., MO(Yoga). His date of joining was 03.11.2018.     
 
2.4.4 With regard to three Medical Officers namely Dr. Ajay Singh Baghel (Homeo), 
Dr.Amit Kumar Verma (Homeo) and Dr. Shakti Singh Parihar (Ayur) who were working on 
contractual basis in AYUSH, they  have been relieved of their services w.e.f. 03.08.2019 
following the dismissal of Misc. Petition No. 4640/2018 (Dr.Amit Kumar Verma& others 
VsAIIMS Bhopal) on 30.07.2019. However, aggrieved with their termination order dated 
27.05.2019 passed by the Institute, they have filed Individual cases 2019 and the Hon‘ble 
CAT Jabalpur was pleased to pass Interim Order dated 29.05.2019 with the direction to stay 
the impugned order dated 27.05.2019 till further orders and the respondents were directed to 
continue the services of the applicant. The Hon‘ble High Court has already dismissed the 
Misc. Petition No. 4640/2018 and all the above named three Medical Officers (Contractual) 
of AYUSH Department were relieved of their services w.e.f. 03.08.2019. AYUSH is 
functional at AIIMS, Bhopal.  The following Six Medical Officers (Regular ) are working in 
the AYUSH Department as per details given below –

B. AIIMS RISHIKESH 
 
2.5 STATUS OF CONSTRUCTION WORK 
Recommendations/Observations  
 
2.5.1 The Committee chides the Rishikesh management in the strongest term for not 
detecting the designing deficiencies in the detailed project report at the initial stage. The 
Committee is distressed to note that the project consu ltants failed miserably in designing the 
blueprint for the Institute and neglected the site specific limitations and concerns related to 
architectural and engineering techniques for a hospital set -up. The Committee is shocked to 
observe that there is no pr ovision for medical gas pipelines system, sewer lines passing 
through operation theatres, basement planning thereby overlooking the water table concerns. 
This was a basic civil engineering concept that was completely overlooked. 
(Para 3.17 of the report) 
Action Taken 
 
2.5.2 It is submitted that work of Medical College gas pipeline system has been completed 
and MGPS running smoothly and supplying medical gas to the hospital.

the reported Rs.150 crore arbitration claims by L&T. The Committee desires that a report on 
the arbitration status along with the position taken by the contractor and the Ministry may also 
be provided so that the matter can be expeditiously resolved without any loss to the 
exchequer. 
 (Para 4.53 of the Report)
